Adic Spaces
Torsten Wedhorn
October 15, 2019
This script is highly preliminary and unﬁnished. It is online only to give
the audience of our lecture easy access to it. Therefore usage is at your own
risk.
Abstract. Adic spaces are objects in the realm of non-archimedean anal ysis and
have been developed by Roland Huber. The goal of these lectur e notes is to give an
introduction to adic spaces.
Acknowledgement. I do not claim any originality here. The main sources are
R. Huber’s works ([Hu1], [Hu2], [Hu3], [Hu4], [HuKn]) and M. Strauch’s introduction
to adic spaces [Str]. Occasionally I follow these sources al most verbatim. The main
example in Section 7.7 follows Section 2 of P. Scholze’s work on perfectoid spaces [Sch].
I am very grateful to R. Huber for sending me a copy of [Hu1] and to M. Strauch for
sending me a copy of [Str]. I am also very grateful for helpful remarks from K. Buzzard,
J. Hilgert, E. Lau, J. Sch¨ utt, and D. Wortmann. J. Sch¨ utt al so provided the proofs
in the sections 2.1–2.3. Special thanks go to B. Conrad who re ad the mansucript very
thoroughly and made many helpful remarks.

1 Valuations
1.1 Totally ordered groups
Deﬁnition 1.1. A totally ordered group (resp. totally ordered monoid ) is a commu-
tative group (resp. commutative monoid) Γ (whose compositi on law is written multi-
plicatively) together with a total order ≤ on Γ such that
γ≤γ′⇒γδ≤γ′δ, for all γ,γ′,δ∈ Γ
Let Γ , Γ′ be totally ordered groups (resp. monoids). A homomorphism o f totally or-
dered groups (resp. totally ordered monoids) is a homomorph ism f : Γ → Γ′ of groups
(resp. monoids) such that for all γ1,γ 2∈ Γ one has γ1≤γ2⇒f (γ1)≤f (γ2).
We obtain the category of totally ordered groups (resp. tota lly ordered monoids).
It is easy to see that a homomorphism is an isomorphism if and o nly if it is bijective.
If Γ is a totally ordered group, γ∈ Γ, and H⊆ Γ a subset, we write γ <H ifγ <δ
for all δ∈H. Similarly we deﬁne “ γ≤H”, “γ >H”, “γ≥H”.
We also deﬁne Γ <γ :={δ∈ Γ ; δ < γ} and have analogous deﬁnitions for Γ ≤γ,
Γ>γ, and Γ ≥γ.
Remark 1.2. Let Γ, Γ′ be totally ordered groups. A group homomorphism f : Γ → Γ′
is a homomorphism of totally ordered groups if and only if for allγ∈ Γ with γ <1 one
has f (γ)< 1.
Example 1.3. (1) R>0 is a totally ordered group with respect to multiplication an d
the standard order. ( R, +) is a totally ordered group w.r.t. the standard order.
The logarithm R>0→ R is an isomorphism of totally ordered groups.
(2) If Γ is a totally ordered group, then every subgroup with t he induced order is again
totally ordered. It is called a totally ordered subgroup .
In particular if M⊆ Γ is a subset, then the intersection of all totally ordered
subgroups of Γ containing M is again a totally ordered subgroup, called the totally
ordered subgroup generated by M .
3
===== PDF PAGE 4 =====
(3) Let I be a well ordered set (e.g. I ={1,...,n } with the standard order) and
let (Γ i)i∈I be a family of totally ordered groups. We endow ∏
i∈I Γi with the
lexicographic order (i.e. ( γi) < (γ′
i) if and only if γj < γ′
j, where j is the smallest
element of I such that γj̸=γ′
j). Then ∏
i∈I Γi is a totally ordered group.
The product order on ∏
i∈I Γi (i.e., (γi)i≤ (γ′
i)i if and only if γi≤γ′
i for alli∈I)
is not a total order (except if there exists only one index i such that Γ i̸={1}).
(4) More generally, let I be a totally ordered index set and let (Γ i)i∈I be a family of
totally ordered groups. We deﬁne the Hahn product HiΓi as the subgroup of those
γ = (γi)i∈ ∏
i Γi such that supp γ :={i∈I ; γi̸= 1} is well-ordered. Then HiΓi
becaomes a totally ordered group if one deﬁnes γ = (γi)i < 1 whenever for the ﬁrst
elementj in the well-ordering of supp γ one has γj < 1.
(5) If Γ is a totally ordered group, then Γ ≤1 and Γ≥1 are totally ordered monoids.
Proposition 1.4. Every totally ordered group is order-isomorphic to a subgro up of a
Hahn product of copies of the totally ordered group R>0 over a suitable totally ordered
set I.
Ordered monoids will mainly come up in the following constru ction.
Remark 1.5. Let Γ be a totally ordered group. We add an element 0 to Γ and
deﬁne on the disjount union Γ ∪{ 0} the structure of a totally ordered monoid as
follows. Restricted to Γ it is the given structure. We extend the multiplication by
0·γ :=γ· 0 := 0 for all γ∈ Γ∪{ 0}. The total order is extended by deﬁning 0 ≤γ for
all γ∈ Γ∪{ 0}. Then Γ ∪{ 0} is a totally ordered monoid.
Let Γ′ be a second totally ordered group. Every isomorphism of tota lly ordered
monoidsf : Γ∪{0} ∼→ Γ′∪{0} sends 0 to 0 and its restriction to Γ yields an isomorphism
Γ ∼→ Γ′ of totally ordered groups. Conversely, every isomorphism Γ → Γ′ of totally
ordered groups can be extended to an isomorphism Γ ∪{0}→ Γ′∪{0} of totally ordered
monoids by sending 0 to 0.
In particular Γ and Γ′ are isomorphic if and only if Γ∪{0} and Γ′∪{0} are isomorphic.
Remark 1.6. Let Γ be a totally ordered group, γ,δ ∈ Γ.
(1) γ <1⇔γ−1> 1.
(2)
(1.1.1) γ,δ ≤ 1⇒γδ≤ 1, γ < 1,δ≤ 1⇒γδ <1,
γ,δ ≥ 1⇒γδ≥ 1, γ > 1,δ≥ 1⇒γδ >1.
(3) Γ is torsion free.
Proof. If δ≤ 1, then γδ≤γ· 1 = γ. Similarly if we replace ≤ by≥. This shows (2).
(1) and (3) are immediate consequences of (2).
Remark and Deﬁnition 1.7. A subgroup ∆ of a totally ordered group Γ is called
isolated or convex if the following equivalent conditions are satisﬁed for all δ,δ′,γ ∈
Γ.
(i) δ≤γ≤ 1 and δ∈ ∆ imply γ∈ ∆.
4
===== PDF PAGE 5 =====
(ii) δ,γ≤ 1 and δγ∈ ∆ imply δ,γ∈ ∆.
(iii) δ≤γ≤δ′ and δ,δ′∈ ∆ imply γ∈ ∆.
Proof. The equivalence of (i) and (iii) is clear. (i)⇒ (ii). As δ,γ ≤ 1 one has
δγ≤ γ≤ 1 and hence γ∈ ∆ by (i). Then δ∈ ∆ because ∆ is a group. (ii)⇒ (i).
Let δ≤γ≤ 1 with δ∈ ∆. Then δγ−1≤ 1 and (δγ−1)γ =δ∈ ∆. This implies γ∈ ∆
by (ii).
Example 1.8. (1) If Γ is a totally ordered group, then 1 and Γ are convex subg roups.
If Γ = R>0 (or any non-trivial subgroup thereof), these are the only co nvex sub-
groups.
(2) Let Γ 1,..., Γn be totally ordered groups and endow Γ = ∏
i Γi with the lexico-
graphic order (where {1,...,n } is well ordered in the standard way). Then for all
r = 1,...,n + 1 the subgroup ∏ n
i=r Γi is a convex subgroup of Γ. If some Γ i for
i> 1 is non- trivial, then Γ 1 is not a convex subgroup of Γ.
Remark 1.9. Let Γ be a totally ordered group, let H be a subgroup. Then the convex
subgroup of Γ generated by H consists of those γ∈ Γ such that there exist h,h′∈H
with h≤γ≤h′ (it is easy to check that this is a subgroup of Γ).
Remark 1.10. Let Γ be a totally ordered group and let ∆, ∆ ′ be convex subgroups.
Then ∆ ⊆ ∆′ or ∆ ′⊆ ∆.
Proof. Assume there exist δ∈ ∆ \ ∆′ and δ′∈ ∆′\ ∆. After possibly replacing these
elements by their inverse we may assume that δ,δ′ < 1. And after possibly swapping
∆ with ∆ ′ we may assume that δ <δ′. But then δ′∈ ∆ because ∆ is convex.
Remark 1.11. Let Γ be a totally ordered group.
(1) If f : Γ → Γ′ is a homomorphism of totally ordered groups, then ker( f ) is a convex
subgroup of Γ.
(2) Let ∆ ⊆ Γ be a convex subgroup and let f : Γ → Γ/∆ be the canonical homomor-
phism. Then there exists a unique total order on Γ /∆ such that f (Γ≤1) = (Γ/∆)≤1.
Then f is a homomorphism of totally ordered groups.
Remark 1.12. Let ∆ ⊆ Γ be a convex subgroup and let f : Γ → Γ/∆ be the canonical
homomorphism. For all γ∈ Γ one then has
f (Γ≥γ) = (Γ/∆)≥γ∆
and hence
f−1((Γ/∆) <γ∆ ) =
⋂

Γ<γδ.
Deﬁnition 1.13. Let Γ be a totally ordered group. The number of convex subgrou ps
̸= 1 of Γ is called the height of Γ: ht Γ ∈ N0∪{∞} .
Clearly ht Γ = 0 if and only if Γ = 1. The height of R>0 or (of any non-trivial
subgroup) is 1.
In fact there is the following converse.
5
===== PDF PAGE 6 =====
Proposition 1.14. For a totally ordered group Γ ̸= 1 the following assertions are
equivalent.
(i) Γ has height 1.
(ii) There exists an injective homomorphism Γ֒→R>0 of totally ordered groups.
(iii) Γ is archimedean, i.e. for all γ,δ ∈ Γ<1 there exists an integer m >0 such that
δm <γ .
Proof. [BouAC] VI, 4.5, Prop. 8.
Remark 1.15. Let ∆ be a convex subgroup of a totally ordered group Γ. Then
ht Γ = ht ∆ + ht Γ /∆
In particular if Γ is the lexicographically ordered product of totally ordered groups ∆
and ∆ ′, then
ht Γ = ht ∆ + ht ∆ ′
Deﬁnition 1.16. Let Γ be a totally ordered group and let H be a subgroup of Γ. We
say that γ∈ Γ∪{ 0} is coﬁnal for H, if for all h∈ H there exists n∈ N such that
γn<h .
Clearly, 0 is coﬁnal for H for every subgroup of Γ, and no γ≥ 1 is coﬁnal for any
subgroup of Γ.
Remark 1.17. Sometimes it is convenient to consider for a totally ordered group Γ
the following topology on Γ ∪{ 0}. A subset U of Γ ∪{ 0} is open if 0 /∈U or if there
exists γ∈ Γ such that Γ <γ⊆U .
Then an element γ∈ Γ is coﬁnal for Γ if and only if 0 is in the closure of {γn ; n∈
N}.
Example 1.18. Let Γ be a totally ordered group of height 1. As Γ is archimedea n
(Proposition 1.14), every element γ∈ Γ with γ <1 is conﬁnal for Γ.
Remark 1.19. Let Γ be a totally ordered group, γ∈ Γ with γ < 1, and let H be a
subgroup of Γ. Then γ is coﬁnal in H if and only if the convex subgroup generated by
γ (Remark 1.9) contains {h∈H ; h≤ 1} (or, equivalently,H).
Indeed, let γ be coﬁnal in H and let h∈ H with h <1. Then there exists n∈ N
such that γn <h< 1 and thus h is contained in the convex subgroup generated by γ.
Conversely, assume that the convex subgroup generated byγ contains{h∈H ; h≤ 1}.
It suﬃces to show that for all h∈ H with h <1 there exists n∈ N with γn < h. By
hypothesis there exists m∈ Z such that γm≤h. One necessarily has m≥ 1 because
γ <1. Then γm+1 <h .
Proposition 1.20. Let Γ be a totally ordered group, Γ′⊆ Γ a convex subgroup, let
γ∈ Γ be coﬁnal for Γ′, and let ∆ ⊊ Γ′ be a proper convex subgroup. Then δγ is coﬁnal
for Γ′ for all δ∈ ∆ .
Proof. Let γ0∈ Γ′ be an element with γ0 < ∆ and hence ∆ <γ−1
0 . Then there exists
n∈ N with γn<γ 0 and hence (δγ)2n <γ−1
0 γ2n<γ n which implies the claim.
Corollary 1.21. Let Γ be a totally ordered group, ∆ ⊊ Γ a proper convex subgroup. If
γ∈ Γ is coﬁnal for Γ , then the image of γ in Γ/∆ is coﬁnal for Γ/∆ .

1.2 Valuations
Deﬁnition 1.22. LetA be a ring. A valuation of A is a map |·| : A→ Γ∪{ 0}, where
Γ is a totally ordered group, such that
(a)|a +b|≤ max(|a|,|b|) for all a,b∈A.
(b)|ab| =|a||b| for all a,b∈A.
(c)|0| = 0 and |1| = 1.
The subgroup of Γ generated by im( |·| )\{ 0} is called the value group of |·| . It is
denoted by Γ|·|.
The set supp(|·| ) :=|·| −1(0) is called the support of|·| .
The second condition shows that|u|∈ Γ for every unit u∈A× and that|·| |A× : A×→
Γ is a homomorphism of groups. Moreover |− 1||− 1| = 1 shows that |− 1| = 1 by 1.6.
Thus we have for all a∈A
|− a| =|a|.
Example 1.23. Let A be a ring and p be a prime ideal of A. Then
a↦→
{
1, a /∈ p;
0, a ∈ p
is a valuation with value group 1. Every valuation on A of this form is called a trivial
valuation.
If F is a ﬁnite ﬁeld, F× is a torsion group. Thus on F there exists only the trivial
valuation.
Remark 1.24. Let|·| be a valuation on A.
(1) Let a,b ∈ A with|a| ̸=|b|. Then |a +b| = max(|a|,|b|). Indeed let |a| <|b|
and assume that |a +b| <|b|. Then |b| =|a +b−a|≤ max(|a +b|,|a|) <|b|.
Contradiction.
(2) Let ϕ : B→A be a homomorphism of rings. Then |·|◦ ϕ is a valuation on B and
supp(|·|◦ ϕ) = ϕ−1(supp(|·| )).
Remark 1.25. Let|·| : A→ Γ∪{ 0} be a valuation of a ring A. Then supp( |·| ) is
a prime ideal in A (and of {a∈ A ; |a|≤ 1}). Denote by K the ﬁeld of fractions of
A/ supp(|·| ). Deﬁne a valuation
|·| ′ : K→ Γ∪{ 0}, ¯a
¯b↦→|a||b|−1,
where ¯a, ¯b∈ A/ supp(|·| ) are the images of elements a,b ∈ A. [Note that for a∈ A,
a′∈supp(|·| ) one has |a +a′| =|a| by 1.24. This and the multiplicativity of |·| shows
that|·| ′ is well deﬁned.]
The support of |·| ′ is the zero ideal and Γ |·| = Γ|·|′.
Conversely let p⊂ A be a prime ideal and let |·| ′ be a valuation on κ(p). Then
its support is the zero ideal. Composing |·| ′ with the canonical ring homomorphism
A→κ(p) we obtain a valuation |·| on A.

===== PDF PAGE 8 =====
Deﬁnition 1.26. Let|·| be a valuation on A. Then
K(|·| ) := Frac(A/ supp(|·| )),
(resp. A(|·| ) :={x∈K(|·| ) ; |x|′≤ 1},
resp. m(|·| ) :={x∈K(|·| ) ; |x|′ < 1},
resp. κ(|·| ) := A(|·| )/m(|·| ) )
is called the valued ﬁeld (resp. valuation ring , resp. the maximal ideal, resp. the residue
ﬁeld ) of|·| .
The height (or rank) of|·| is deﬁned as the height of the value group of |·| .
Proposition and Deﬁnition 1.27. Two valuations |·| 1 and|·| 2 on a ring A are
called equivalent if the following equivalent conditions are satisﬁed.
(i) There exists an isomorphism of totally ordered monoid f : Γ|·|1∪{ 0} ∼→ Γ|·|2∪{ 0}
such that f◦|·| 1 =|·| 2.
(ii) supp(|·| 1) = supp(|·| 2) and A(|·| 1) = A(|·| 2).
(iii) For all a,b∈A one has |a|1≤|b|1 if and only if |a|2≤|b|2.
Note that f in (i) restricts to an isomorphism Γ|·|1
∼→ Γ|·|2 of totally ordered groups.
Proof. All three conditions imply that supp( |·| 1) = supp(|·| 2) =: p. For a∈ A the
valuations|a|1 and|a|2 depend only on the image of a inA/p. Thus we may replace A
byA/p. Then A is an integral domain, supp( |·| i) = 0 for i = 1, 2, and we can extend
|·| 1 and|·| 2 to the ﬁeld of fractions K of A. Thus we may assume that |·| 1 and|·| 2
are valuations on a ﬁeld.
Clearly, (i) implies (iii) and (iii) implies (ii). If (ii) ho lds, then also {a∈K ;|a|1≥
1} ={a∈ K ; |a|2 ≥ 1} and hence |·| 1 : K× → Γ|·|1 and|·| 2 : K× → Γ|·|2 are
surjective group homomorphisms with the same kernel. Thus t here exists a unique
group homomorphism f : Γ|·|1
∼→ Γ|·|2
such that f◦|·| 1 =|·| 2. It maps elements ≤ 1
to elements ≤ 1 and thus is a homomorphism of totally ordered groups, and we can
extend f to Γ|·|1∪{ 0} by setting f (0) = 0.
Remark 1.28. Let A be a ring. For a valuation |·| denote by [|·| ] its equivalence
class. It follows from 1.25 that |·|↦→ (supp(|·| ),|·| ′) yields a bijection
(1.2.1) { [|·| ] ; |·| valuation on A}
↔{ (p, [|·| ′]) ; p∈ SpecA,|·| ′ valuation on FracA/p}.

the group Rr

is a valuation on A.

Example 1.30. Every totally ordered group Γ is the value group of a valuatio n which
can be constructed as follows. Let k be any ﬁeld and set C := k[Γ≤1] (the monoid
algebra of the monoid of elements ≤ 1 in Γ). By deﬁnition C has a k-basis (xγ)γ∈Γ ≤1
and its multiplication is given by xγxδ =xγδ . Deﬁne a valuation on C by|0| = 0 and

aγxγ

⏐ := sup{γ ; aγ̸= 0}.
Then|·| is a valuation on C with value group Γ and support 0.
2 Valuation rings
2.1 Deﬁnition of valuation rings
Remark 2.1. LetA andB local rings with A⊆B. Then we say that B dominates A
if mB∩A = mA. Given a ﬁeld K, the set of local subrings of K is inductively ordered
with respect to domination order.
Proposition 2.2. LetK be a ﬁeld and let A be a subring of K. The following conditions
are equivalent.
(i) For every a∈K× one has a∈A or a−1∈A.
(ii) Frac A =K and the set of ideals of A is totally ordered by inclusion.
(iii) A is local and a maximal element in the set of local subrings of K with respect to
the domination order.
(iv) There exists a valuation |·| on K such that A =A(|·| ).
(v) There exists an algebraically closed ﬁeld L and a ring homomorphism h : A→ L
which is maximal in the set of homomorphisms from subrings of K to L (here we
deﬁne h≤ h′ for homomorphisms h : A→ L and h′ : A′→ L, where A, A′ are
subrings of K, if A⊆A′ and h =h′|A).
Proof. “(i) ⇔ (iv)”. Clearly (iv) implies (i). Conversely if (i) holds, it is easy to check
that
|·| : K→K×/A×∪{ 0}, |x| =
{
0, if x = 0;
[x] := x mod A×, if x∈K×,
deﬁnes a valuation, where K×/A× is ordered by [ x]≤ [y] :⇔xy−1∈A.
“(i)⇒ (ii)”. FracA =K is obvious. Let a, b⊆A be ideals. If a is not a subset of
b and a∈ a\b, then b−1a /∈ A holds for every b∈ b and thus a−1b∈ A. This implies
Ab⊆Aa for all b∈ b and in particular b⊆ a.
“(ii)⇒ (iii)”. As the ideals of A are totally ordered by inclusion, A has to be
local. Denote by mA its maximal ideal. Let B⊆ K be a subring dominating A. For
x∈B\{0} we can write x =ab−1 with a,b∈A. We have to show that x∈A. In case
Aa⊆ Ab it directly follows x∈ A. On the other hand if Ab⊆ Aa we have x−1∈ A.
But x−1 /∈ mA since B dominates A. This implies x∈A×.
“(iii)⇒ (v)”. Let κ(A) be the residue ﬁeld of A, let L be an algebraic closure
of κ(A), and let h : A→ L the canonical homomorphism. Further let A′⊆ K be a
9
===== PDF PAGE 10 =====
subring containing A and let h′ : A′→ L be an extension of h. For p = kerh′ we get
p∩A = mA. Hence A′
p dominates AmA =A, which shows A =A′
p and thus A′ =A.
“(v)⇒ (i)”. For this implication we will use the following easy remark: L etR be a
ring,B anR-algebra, and b∈B. Then b is integral over A if and only if b−1∈A[b−1]×.
Now we are able to prove that (v) implies (i). First we show tha t A is local with
mA = kerh. If h(x)̸= 0 for x∈A we get a natural extension of h toAx. By maximality
of h it then follows Ax =A and thus x∈A×.
Now let x∈ K be integral over A. Then A′ := A[x] is a ﬁnite extension of A and
there exists a maximal ideal m′ of A′ so that m′∩A = mA. Hence A′/m′ is a ﬁnite
extension of A/m and because L is algebraically closed there exists an extension of h
to A′/m′ and thereby to A′. Thus A =A′ and x∈A.
Now, if x∈K is not integral over A we have x−1 /∈A[x−1]× by the above remark.
Hence there exists a maximal ideal m′ of A[x−1] containing x−1. Since A→A[x−1]→
A[x−1]/m′ is surjective, its kernel must be mA and we obtain a natural extension of h
to A[x−1]. Therefore A[x−1] = A and x−1∈A.
Deﬁnition 2.3. An integral domain A with ﬁeld of fractions K is called valuation ring
(of K) if K and A satisfy the equivalent conditions in Prop. 2.2.
Remark 2.4. If A is a valuation ring, then up to equivalence there exists a uni que
valuation|·| onK := FracA such that A =A(|·| ) (Proposition 1.27). Its value group
Γ|·| is isomorphic Γ A :=K×/A× with the total order deﬁned by xA×≤yA× :⇔xy−1∈
A. The valuation is trivial if and only if A =K.
Remark 2.5. Let A be a valuation ring, K := FracA, k :=A/mA, and let π : A→k
be the canonical map. Then the map
{B ; B valuation ring of k}→{ ˜B ; ˜B valuation ring of K contained in A},
B↦→π−1(B)
is (well deﬁned and) bijective.
2.2 Examples of valuation rings
Lemma 2.6. LetA be a local ring whose maximal ideal is generated by one elemen t p.
Assume that ⋂
n≥1Apn = 0 (which is automatic if A is noetherian). Deﬁne
v : A→ Z∪{∞}, v (x) :=
{
n, if x∈Apn\Apn+1;
∞, otherwise.
Then the only ideals of A are 0 and Apn forn≥ 0. Moreover p is either nilpotent or A
is a valuation ring and its valuation is given by x↦→γv(x), where γ is any real number
with 0<γ < 1.
Proof. Since ⋂
n≥1Apn=0 we have v(x) =∞ if and only if x = 0. Let a be a non-zero
ideal of A and a∈ a such that v(a) is minimal. Clearly a⊆ Apv(a). Furthermore,
there exists an u∈ A with a = upv(a) and clearly v(u) = 0, i.e., u∈ A×. This shows
10
===== PDF PAGE 11 =====
pv(a)∈ Aa and thus a = Apv(a). As we have seen that every element a∈ A can be
written as a =upv(a) with u∈A×, it also follows that if p is not nilpotent, A will be
an integral domain with valuation v.
Proposition 2.7. LetA be an integral domain which is not a ﬁeld. Then the following
assertions are equivalent.
(i) A is a noetherian valuation ring.
(ii) A is a local principal domain.
(iii) A is a valuation ring and ΓA is isomorphic to the totally ordered group Z.
(iv) A is local, its maximal ideal m is a principal ideal, and ⋂
n>0 mn = 0.
Proof. “(iii) ⇒ (ii),(i)”. Without loss of generality we may assume that Γ A is the
(additive) group Z. Let π∈A with|π| = 1. For x∈A we have|x| =n =|πn| which
impliesx =uπn for a unit u∈A×. Now (ii) and (i) follow as in the proof of Lemma 2.6.
“(ii) ⇒ (iv)”. Let mA = Aπ. Then π is the only prime element of A up to
multiplication with units of A and every non-zero ideal has the form Aπn. As A is
factorial, the only element of A dividing all powers of the prime element π is 0, i.e.,⋂
n≥1 mn
A = 0.
“(iv)⇒ (iii)”. This follows directly from Lemma 2.6 with v as valuation.
“(i)⇒ (ii)”. Let a⊆ A be an ideal generated by elements x1,...,x n∈ A. Since
the ideals xiA are totally ordered by inclusion, a is generated by one of the elements
xi. Thus A is a principal domain.
Deﬁnition 2.8. Let A be an integral domain which is not a ﬁeld. If the equivalent
properties of Prop. 2.7 are satisﬁed, A is called a discrete valuation ring and its valuation
is called a discrete valuation. It is called normed if its value group is Z.
Example 2.9. Let A be a factorial ring, K = FracA. Let p∈A be a prime element.
Letvp : K→ Z∪{∞} be the p-adic valuation. Fix a real number 0 <ρ< 1 and deﬁne
|x| :=ρvp(x) for x∈K. Then |·| p is a valuation (also called the p-adic valuation). Its
valuation ring is A(p). Its equivalence class does not depend on ρ.
Example 2.10. Let A be a principal ideal domain and K its ﬁeld of fractions. The
valuation rings of K containingA and distinct from K are the rings A(p), where p is a
prime element of A.
In particular, every valuation ring of the ﬁeld Q and distinct from Q is of the form
Z(p) (because every subring of Q contains Z).
Example 2.11. Let k be a ﬁeld. The only valuations on k(T ) whose restriction to k
is trivial are (up to equivalence) the p- adic valuations, where p∈k[T ] is an irreducible
polynomial, and the valuation f/g↦→ρdeg(g)−deg(f ) forf,g ∈k[T ],g̸= 0 and 0 <ρ< 1
a real number.

Proposition 2.12. LetK be a ﬁeld, let |·| be a valuation on K, A its valuation ring,

Remark 2.13. Let K be a ﬁeld and let A be a valuation ring of K. Then every ring

2.4 Extension of valuation
Proposition 2.15. LetK be a ﬁeld, let |·| be a valuation on K, and let K′ be a ﬁeld
extension of K. Then there exists a valuation |·| ′ on K′ whose restriction to K is

Proof. [BouAC] VI,§2.4, Prop. 4.
Proposition 2.16. LetK be a ﬁeld, let K′⊃K be an algebraic extension, let |·| ′ be
a valuation on K′ and let |·| be its restriction to K. Let k (resp. k′) be the residue
ﬁeld of A(|·| ) (resp.A(|·| ′)).
(1) Γ |·|′/Γ|·| is a torsion group and ht(Γ|·|′) = ht(Γ|·|).
(2) k′ is an algebraic extension of k.
(3)|·| is trivial (resp. of height 1) if and only if |·| ′ is trivial (resp. of height 1).

Proof. [BouAC] VI,§8.

In general, an extension of a valuation on a ﬁeld K to a ﬁeld extension K′ is not
unique. In fact, this is never the case if K′ is transcendental over K as the second
assertion of Prop. 2.15 shows. But even if K′ is an algebraic extension we need some
additional hypothesis. In fact this is closely connected to Hensel’s lemma. Thus there
is the following deﬁnition.
Proposition and Deﬁnition 2.17. LetA be a local ring, k its residue ﬁeld. Then A
is called henselian if the following equivalent conditions are satisﬁed.
(i) Every ﬁnite A-algebra is a (necessarily ﬁnite) product of local rings.
(ii) For every monic polynomial F ∈ A[T ] with image F0 ∈ k[T ] and for every de-
composition F0 = G0H0 into polynomials G0,H 0 ∈ k[T ] that are prime to each
other, there exists a unique pair (G,H ) of monic polynomials in A[T ] such that:
G0 andH0 are the images of G andH, respectively,F =GH, and the ideal of A[T ]
generated by G and H is A[T ].
(iii) For every smooth morphism of schemes f : X→ SpecA the canonical mapX(A)→
X(k) is surjective (where, with S = Spec A, X(A) = Hom S(S,X ) and X(k) =
HomS(Speck,X ) = Homk(Speck,X⊗Ak)).
Proof. [EGA] IV (18.5.11), (18.5.13), (18.5.17).
Proposition 2.18. Let|·| be a valuation on a ﬁeld K. Then A(|·| ) is henselian if
and only if for every algebraic extension K′ of K any two extensions of |·| to K′ are
equivalent.
If these equivalent conditions are satisﬁed, A(|·| ′) is also henselian.
Proof. See e.g. [FuSa] II, Theorem 7.3
Remark 2.19. Let|·| be a valuation on a ﬁeld K. By Hensel’s lemma, if A(|·| ) is
complete and|·| is of height 1, then A(|·| ) is henselian.
But there exist valuations of height 2 such that A(|·| ) is complete but there exist two
non-equivalent extensions to a quadratic extension of K ([BouAC] VI,§8, Exercise 1).
In particular A(|·| ) is not henselian.
For transcendental extensions we extend valuations almost “arbitrarily”:

be a totally ordered group containing Γ and let γ∈ Γ′. Then there exists a valuation
|·| ′ on K(T ) with values in Γ′ such that |T|′ =γ.

3 Spectral spaces
Similarly as general schemes are obtained by gluing aﬃne sch emes, adic spaces will be
obtained by gluing aﬃnoid adic spaces. In both cases the unde rlying topological spaces
are spectral spaces, which ﬁrst have been singled out by Hoch ster ([Ho]).
3.1 Sober spaces
We ﬁrst recall the deﬁnition of irreducible spaces.
Remark and Deﬁnition 3.1. Let X be a non-empty topological space. Then X is
called irreducible if X satisﬁes one of the following equivalent conditions.
(i) X cannot be expressed as the union of two proper closed subsets .
(ii) Every non-empty open subset of X is dense.
(iii) Any two non-empty open subsets of X have non-empty intersection.
A non-empty subset Z of X is called irreducible if Z is irreducible when we endow it
with the induced topology.
Lemma 3.2. Let X be a topological space. A subspace Y ⊆ X is irreducible if and
only if its closure
Y is irreducible.
Proof. A subset Z of X is irreducible if and only if for any two open subsets U and V
ofX withZ∩U̸=∅ andZ∩V̸=∅ we have Z∩ (U∩V )̸=∅. This implies the lemma
because an open subset meets Y if and only if it meets Y .
Remark 3.3. In particular, a subset of the form {x} for x∈X is always irreducible.
Remark 3.4. Let X be a topological space. If U⊆X is an open subset and Z⊆X
is irreducible and closed, Z∩U is open in Z. Hence if Z∩U̸=∅, then Z∩U is an
irreducible closed subset of U whose closure in X is Z. Together with Lemma 3.2 this
shows that there are mutually inverse bijective maps
(3.1.1)
{Y ⊆U irreducible closed}↔{ Z⊆X irreducible closed with Z∩U̸=∅}
Y ↦→
Y (closure in X)
Z∩U←↦Z
Remark and Deﬁnition 3.5. An irreducible subset that is maximal w.r.t. inclusion
is called an irreducible component.
The set of irreducibly subsets is inductively ordered. Henc e every irreducible subset
is contained in an irreducible component. In particular X is the union of its irreducible
components. By Lemma 3.2 irreducible components are always closed.
A pointx∈X is called a maximal point if its closure{x} is an irreducible component
of X.
Deﬁnition 3.6. Let X be an arbitrary topological space.
(1) A point x∈X is called closed if the set {x} is closed,
(2) We say that a point η∈X is a generic point if{η} =X.
16
===== PDF PAGE 17 =====
(3) Let x and x′ be two points of X. We say that x is a generization of x′ or that x′
is a specialization of x if x′∈{x}. We write x⪰ x′ or x′⪯ x.
Deﬁnition 3.7. A topological space X is called sober if every irreducible closed subset
of X has a unique generic point.
Remark 3.8. Recall that a topological space X is called Kolmogorov (orT0) if for any
two distinct points there exists an open set containing one o f the points but not the
other.
A topological space is Kolmogorov if and only if every closed irreducible subset
admits at most one generic point. Indeed, for x,y ∈X with x̸=y one has{x}̸={y}
if and only if there exists a closed subset containing one of t he points but not the
other one. By taking complements this is equivalent to the ex istence of an open subset
containing one point but not the other.
In particular we see that every sober space is Kolmogorov.
Remark 3.9. IfX is a sober space (or, more generally, a topological space whe re every
closed irreducible subset has at most one generic point), th en⪯ is a partial order on
X.
Example 3.10. (1) If X is a Hausdorﬀ space, then the only irreducible subsets of X
are the sets {x} for x∈X.
(2) Let Z be an inﬁnite set endowed with the topology such that the clos ed sets ̸=Z
are all ﬁnite subsets. Then Z is irreducible but has no generic point. In particular
Z is not sober. If we add a single point η without changing the closed subsets
̸=Z∪{η}, then Z∪{η} is sober.
In particular we see that there are subspaces of sober spaces which are not sober.
(3) Let X be a topological space in which X and∅ are the only open subsets. Then
every point of X is a generic point.
3.2 Spectral spaces
Deﬁnition 3.11. Let X be a topological space and let f : X→ Y be a continuous
map of topological spaces.
(1) X is called quasi-separated if the intersection of any two quasi-compact open subsets
is again quasi-compact.
(2) A subspace Z ofX is called retro-compact if the inclusion Z→X is quasi-compact.
(3) f is called quasi-compact if for every open quasi-compact subset V ofY the inverse
image f−1(V ) is quasi-compact.
(4) f is called quasi-separated if for every open quasi-separated subset V of Y the
inverse image f−1(V ) is quasi-separated.
We abbreviate the property “quasi-compact and quasi-separ ated” to “qcqs”.
Deﬁnition 3.12. A topological space X is called spectral if it satisﬁes the following
conditions.
(a) X is quasi-compact.
17
===== PDF PAGE 18 =====
(b) X has a basis consisting of quasi-compact open subsets which i s closed under ﬁnite
intersections.
(c) X is sober.
X is called locally spectral if X has an open covering by spectral spaces.
Remark 3.13. (1) Any locally spectral space X is sober and in particular Kolmogorov
(Remark 3.8).
(2) A locally spectral space is spectral if and only if it is qc qs.
(3) A ﬁnite sum of spectral spaces is again spectral.
(4) Every quasi-compact open subspace U of a spectral space is again spectral: By ((2)),
U inherits from X a basis of quasi-compact open subsets which is closed under
intersection. By (3.1.1) any open subspace of a sober space i s again sober.
(5) Every closed subspace of a spectral space is again spectr al: This is clear as any
closed subspace of a quasi- compact is again quasi-compact a nd every closed sub-
space of sober space is sober.
(6) A locally spectral space X has a basis consisting of spectral open subspaces and X
is sober. Note that Example 3.16 below shows that it may happe n that such a basis
is not stable under ﬁnite intersections (in oher words, X is not quasi-separated).
(7) Every open subspace of a locally spectral space is locall y spectral.
Example 3.14. Let A be a ring and endow Spec A :={ p ; p prime ideal} with the
usual topology (i.e. the closed sets of Spec A are of the form V (a) :={ p ; a⊆ p} for
ideal a of A). Then Spec A is spectral: For f ∈ A set D(f ) = { p ; f /∈ p}. Then
the (D(f ))f∈A form a basis of open quasi-compact subsets of Spec A stable under ﬁnite
intersections.
For two prime ideals p and q of A, p is a specialization of q (or, equivalently, q is a
generization of p) if and only if q⊆ p. For every irreducible closed subset Z of Spec A
there exists a unique prime ideal p∈Z such that p⊆ q for all q∈Z.
Let X be a scheme. Then the underlying topological space of X is spectral if and
only if X is qcqs.
In fact, Hochster has shown in [Ho] the converse.
Theorem 3.15. (1) For a topological space X the following assertions are equiva-
lent.
(i) X is spectral.
(ii) X is homeomorphic to SpecA for some ring A.
(iii) X is homeomorphic to the underlying topological space of a qua si-compact
quasi-separated scheme.
(iv) X is the projective limit of ﬁnite Kolmogorov spaces.
(2) A topological space is locally spectral if and only it is home omorphic to the under-
lying topological space of a scheme.
(3) For every continuous quasi-compact map f : X′→X between spectral spaces there
exists a ring homomorphism ϕ : A→A′ such that the associated continuous map is
f .
18
===== PDF PAGE 19 =====
Example 3.16. Set X := N∪{∞} endowed with the usual (total) order and deﬁne
its set of open subsets by {X<x ; x∈X}∪{ X}.
The only open subset containing ∞ is X itself, in particular X is quasi-compact.
For all x∈ X the set X≤x is open quasi-compact and these sets form a basis of the
topoology stable under ﬁnite intersection. The non-empty c losed subsets are the sets
X≥x for x∈X. All of them are irreducible with unique generic point x. Thus X is a
spectral space.
In fact, X is the spectrum of a valuation ring with value group ∏
N Z (ordered
lexicographically).
Note that X<∞ is open and not quasi-compact. If we glue two copies of X along
X<∞, then the resulting space is locally spectral and quasi-com pact but it is not quasi-
separated and in particular not spectral.
3.3 Constructible topology
Deﬁnition 3.17. LetX be a spectral space.
(1) A subset Z of X is called constructible if it is in the Boolean algebra C of subsets
of X that is generated by all open quasi-compact subsets (i.e. C is the smallest
set of subsets of X that contains all open quasi-compact subsets, is stable und er
ﬁnite intersections and under taking complements (this imp lies thatC is also stable
under ﬁnite unions)).
(2) A subset Z of X is called pro-constructible (resp. ind-constructible) if it is an
intersection (resp. a union) of constructible subsets.
If X is a locally spectral space, a subset Z of X is called constructible (resp. pro-
constructible, resp. ind-constructible) if there exists an open covering ( Ui)i by spec-
tral spaces such that Z∩ Ui is a constructible (resp. pro-constructible, resp. ind-
constructible) subset of Ui for all i∈I.
Using the quasi-compactness of spectral spaces, it is immed iate that the second
deﬁnition of constructible subsets in locally spectral spa ces X coincides with the ﬁrst
one if X is spectral.
Lemma 3.18. LetX be a spectral space. Then a subset Z is constructible if and only
if it is a ﬁnite union of subsets of the form U\V , where U and V are quasi-compact
open subsets of X.
Proof. This is a special case of the following general principle: Le t X be any set and
letB be a Boolean algebra generated by a set S of subsets of X such thatS is stable
under ﬁnite intersections and ﬁnite unions. Then B is the set of ﬁnite unions of subsets
of the form U\V with U,V ∈S .
Indeed, clearly every such set U\V is inB. So it suﬃces to show that the complement
of any ﬁnite union of subsets U\V as above is again such a ﬁnite union. But if
C = ⋃
i∈IUi\Vi is a ﬁnite union, Ui,Vi∈S , then, denoting complements by −c,
Cc =
⋂
i
Uc
i∪Vi

is the union of all sets of the form
( ⋂

)
, J ⊆I.
As ﬁnite unions and ﬁnite intersections of subsets in S are again in S, Cc is again of
the desired form.
Proposition 3.19. LetX be a ﬁnite Kolmogorov space.
(1) X is spectral.
(2) Every subset of X is constructible.
Proof. (1). Clearly, any open subset of X is quasi-compact. Thus it remains to show
that any closed irreducible subset Z has a generic point. Assume that this is not the
case, i.e. for all z∈Z, Uz :=Z\{z} is a non-empty open subset of Z not containing
z. As Z is ﬁnite and irreducible, one has ∅̸= ⋂
zUz. But by deﬁnition, one clearly has⋂
zUz =∅. Contradiction.
(2). Every subset of a ﬁnite Kolmogorov space is locally closed.
Corollary 3.20. LetX be a spectral space.
(1) Every constructible subspace is quasi-compact.
(2) An open (resp. a closed) subset Z of X is constructible if and only if Z is quasi-
compact (resp. X\Z is quasi-compact).
Proof. Clearly (1) implies (2). To show (1) it suﬃces by Lemma 3.18 to show that if
U ,V are open quasi-compact, then U\V is quasi-compact. But U\V is closed in the
quasi-compact subset U . Hence it is quasi-compact.
Remark 3.21. Let X be a locally spectral space.
(1) A subset Z ofX is pro-constructible if and only if its complement is ind-co nstructible.
(2) Finite unions and arbitrary intersections of pro-const ructible subsets are pro-constructible.
Finite intersections and arbitrary unions of ind-construc tible subsets are ind-constructible.
(3) Every pro-constructible subset of X is retro-compact in X. Conversely, a locally
closed subset of X is retro-compact if and only if it is pro-constructible.
(4) Let ( Ui)i be an open covering of X. Then a subset Z of X is pro-constructible
(resp. ind-constructible) if and only if Z∩Ui is pro-constructible (resp. ind-constructible)
for all i∈I.
Proof. The ﬁrst two assertions are clear. For the other assertions w e may assume by
Hochster’s Theorem 3.15 that X is the underlying topological space of a scheme. Then
all assertions follow from [EGA] I (7.2.3)
In particular we see that the ind-constructible (resp. pro- constructible) subsets are
the open (resp. closed) subsets of a topology on X:
Deﬁnition 3.22. Let X be a locally spectral space. The topology on X where the
open subsets are the ind-constructible subsets is called th e constructible topology. X
endowed with the constructible topology is denoted by Xcons.
20
===== PDF PAGE 21 =====
Proposition 3.23. LetX be a locally spectral space, T its topology and Tcons its con-
structible topology.
(1) The topologyTcons is ﬁner thanT (i.e., every closed subset of X is pro-constructible).
(2) The constructible subsets are those subsets which are open an d closed in the con-
structible topology (i.e., Z⊆X is constructible if and only if Z is pro-constructible
and ind-constructible).
(3) For every open subspace U of X, the topology of Tcons induced on U is the same as
the topology of Ucons (i.e. the inclusion is an open continuous map Ucons→Xcons.
(4) Xcons is quasi-compact if and only if X is quasi-compact.
(5) Xcons is Hausdorﬀ if and only if X is quasi-separated. In this case Xcons is locally
compact and totally disconnected.
In particular if X is a spectral space, then Xcons is a compact totally disconnected
space.
Proof. By Hochster’s Theorem 3.15 we may assume thatX is the underlying topological
space of a scheme. Then all assertions follow from [EGA] I (7. 2.12) and (7.2.13).
Corollary 3.24. Let X be a quasi-compact locally spectral space, (Zi)i∈I a family of
pro-constructible subsets of X and let W be an ind-constructible subset of X such that⋂
i∈IZi⊆W . Then there exists a ﬁnite subset J of I such that ⋂
j∈JZj⊆W .
Proof. The complement of W is closed in the quasi-compact space Xcons and hence
W is quasi-compact in Xcons. Thus already a ﬁnite number of complements of the Zi
(which are open in Xcons) suﬃce to cover the complement of W .
Lemma 3.25. LetX be a locally spectral space.
(1) Letf : X′→X be a qcqs map of locally spectral spaces. If Z is a pro-constructible
(resp. ind-constructible) subset of X, then f−1(Z) is a pro-constructible (resp. ind-
constructible) subset of X′.
If Z′ is a pro-constructible subset of X′, then f (Z′) is pro-constructible.
(2) Assume that X is spectral. Then conversely a subset Z of X is pro-constructible if
and only if there exists a spectral space X′ and a quasi-compact map f : X′→ X
such that Z =f (X′).
Proof. To prove (1) we may assume that X is spectral. Then X′ is qcqs and hence
spectral.
Thus for the proof of all assertions we may assume that f : X′→X is associated to a
homomorphism of rings and the assertions are [EGA] I (7.2.1) and [EGA] I (7.2.3) (vii).
Deﬁnition 3.26. A continuous map f : X → Y of locally spectral spaces is called
spectral if for all open spectral subspaces V ⊆ Y and U ⊆ f−1(V ) the restriction
f : U→V is quasi-compact.
Proposition 3.27. Let f : X→ Y be a continuous map of spectral spaces. Then the
following assertions are equivalent.
21
===== PDF PAGE 22 =====
(i) f is spectral.
(ii) f : Xcons→Ycons is continuous.
(iii) f is quasi-compact.
(iv) The inverse image of every constructible subset is construct ible.
(v) The inverse image of every pro-constructible subset is pro-c onstructible.
If these equivalent conditions are satisﬁed, then f : Xcons→Ycons is proper. In partic-
ular the images of pro-constructible subsets are again pro- constructible.
Proof. The implications “(i) ⇒ (iii)⇒ (iv)⇒ (v)⇔ (ii)” are clear. Moreover, (ii)
implies (iii) because the open quasi-compact subsets of X are those subsets that are
open in X and open and closed in Xcons.
Thus to show that all assertions are equivalent it suﬃces to s how that (iii) implies (i).
But if f is quasi-compact, by Hochster’s theorem we may assume that f is associated
to a ring homomorphism. Then the restriction of f to open spectral subspaces U and
V is the underlying topological map of a morphism of schemes be tween qcqs schemes
and hence it is quasi-compact.
The last assertion is clear as any continuous map between com pact spaces is proper
(see below).
Recall that a continuous map f : X → Y of topological spaces is called proper
([BouTG] Chap. I §10) if it satisﬁes the following equivalent properties.
(i) For every topological space Z the map f× idZ : X×Z→Y×Z is closed.
(ii) f is closed and for all y∈Y the ﬁber f−1(y) is quasi-compact.
Example 3.28. Letϕ : A→B be a homomorphism of rings. Then the corresponding
scheme morphism Spec B→ SpecA is quasi-compact and hence spectral.
3.4 Construction of spectral spaces
Lemma 3.29. LetX be a quasi-compact Kolmogorov space which has a basis consis ting
of open quasi-compact subsets which is stable under ﬁnite in tersections. Let X′ be the
topological space with the same underlying set as X and whose topology is generated
by the open quasi-compact subsets of X and their complements. Then the following
assertions are equivalent.
(i) X is spectral.
(ii) X′ is compact with a basis of open and closed subspaces.
(iii) X′ is quasi-compact.
Proof. Assertion (i) implies (ii) by Proposition 3.23 and “(ii) ⇒ (iii)” is clear. Let us
show that (iii) implies (i). Let Z be a closed irreducible subspace of X and denote by
Z′ the subspace of X′ with the same underlying set. As the topology of X′ is ﬁner than
the topology of X, Z′ is closed in X′ and hence quasi-compact.
Assume that Z has no generic point. Then
{z} is a proper subspace of Z for all
z∈ Z. By hypothesis there exists a non-empty open quasi-compact subset Uz of Z
that does not meet {z}. In particular Z = ⋃
z(Z\Uz). By hypothesis Z′\Uz is open
inZ′ and as Z′ is quasi-compact, there exist ﬁnitely many of the sets Z\Uz that cover
Z. This contradicts the irreducibility of Z.
22
===== PDF PAGE 23 =====
Proposition 3.30. LetX be a spectral space and let Z be a subspace.
(1) Z is pro-constructible if and only if Z is spectral and the inclusion Z → X is
spectral.
(2) LetZ be a pro-constructible subspace of X. The closure of Z is the set of special-
izations of points of Z.
Proof. (1). The condition is suﬃcient by the last assertion of Propositi on 3.27. The
inclusion Z→ X is quasi-compact by Remark 3.21 (3). Thus Z is a quasi-compact
Kolmogorov space and admits a basis by open quasi-compact su bsets stable under ﬁnite
intersections. With the induced topology of Xcons the subspaceZ is compact. Therefore
Z is spectral by Lemma 3.29, and the inclusion Z→X is spectral by Proposition 3.27.
(2). We have to show that if x∈Z then there exists z∈Z with x∈{z}. Set
F :={U ; U quasi-compact open neigborhood of x}∪{ Z}.
Then any ﬁnite intersection of sets in F is non-empty. Moreover all sets in F are closed
subsets of the compact space Xcons. Thus the intersection of all quasi-compact open
neigborhoods of x in X with Z contains a point z. Thus x∈{z}.
The following proposition will allow us to construct spectr al spaces.
Proposition 3.31. Let X′ = (X0,T′) be a quasi-compact space, let U be a set of
open and closed subspaces, let T be the topology generated by U , and set X = (X0,T ).
Assume that X is Kolmogorov. Then X is a spectral space, U is a basis of open quasi-
compact subsets of X, and Xcons =X′.
Proof. We may replaceU by the set of ﬁnite intersections of sets in U . As the topology
onX is coarser than the topology of X′, every quasi-compact subset of X′ is also quasi-
compact in X. Every set in U is closed in X′ and thus quasi-compact in X. Thus X
is quasi-compact and U is by construction a basis of open quasi-compact subsets of X
stable under ﬁnite intersection. Moreover X is Kolmogorov by hypothesis.
By Lemma 3.29 it remains to show that the topology T′′ generated by the open
quasi-compact subsets of X and their complements is equal to T′. But T′′ is Hausdorﬀ
(as X is Kolmogorov there exists for x̸= y an U ∈U with, say, x∈ U and y /∈ U ;
thenU andX′\U are open sets of T′′ separatingx andy) and coarser then T′. Hence
id :X′→ X′′ := (X0,T′′) is continuous and hence a homeomorphism because X′ is
quasi-compact and X′′ is Hausdorﬀ.

4.2 Specializations in valuation spectra
Remark 4.10. Let v,w ∈ SpvA such that v is a specialization of w. Then supp v⊇

Proposition 4.11. LetK be a ﬁeld and let v and w be valuations on K. Then v is a
specialization of w if and only if A(v)⊆A(w).

⇔ (∀ f,s∈A : v∈ Spv(A)(f
s )⇒w∈ Spv(A)(f
s )).

To consider specialization with changing support we need th e following notion.
Deﬁnition 4.13. Let v be a valuation on a ring A and let Γ v its value group. Then
the convex subgroup of Γ v generated by the elements Γ v,≥1∩ im(v) is called the char-
acteristic group of v. It is denoted by cΓv.
Hence cΓv = 1 if and only if v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈A.
Example 4.14. (1) Let v be a valuation on a ﬁeld. Then im( v)\{ 0} = Γ v and
cΓv = Γv.
(2) Let A be a valuation ring and letv : A→ Γv∪{0} its valuation. Then Γ v,≥1∩im(v) =
{1} and cΓv = 1.
Remark 4.15. Let v be a valuation on a ring A. Let H⊆ Γv be a subgroup. Deﬁne
a map
|·| v| H : A→H∪{ 0}, f ↦→
{
|f|v, if|f|v∈H;
0, if|f|v /∈H.
Then v|H is a valuation if and only if H is a convex subgroup and contains cΓv.
Indeed, one always has v|H(0) = 0, v|H(1) = 1. Assume that v|H is a valuation.
Then|fg|v| H =|f|v|H|g|v| H is equivalent to|f|v|g|v∈H⇒|f|v,|g|v∈H, in particular
H is a convex subgroup. Moreover if there existed f∈A with|f|v > 1 and |f|v /∈H,
then|f + 1|v = max{|f|v, 1} =|f|v /∈H and hence 0 = |f + 1|v| H = max{|f|v| H, 1} = 1,
contradiction.
Conversely it is easy to see that if H is convex and contains cΓv, then v|H is a
valuation.
Remark and Deﬁnition 4.16. Let v be a valuation on a ring A with value group
Γv, let ϕ : A→ K(v) = Frac( A/ supp(v)) be the canonical homomorphism, and let
A(v)⊆K(v) be the valuation ring of K(v). Let H be a convex subgroup of Γ v and let
p be the corresponding prime ideal of A(v), i.e., p ={x∈A(v) ; ∀δ∈H :|x|v <δ }.
Then cΓv⊆H if and only if ϕ(A)⊆A(v)p. Let this be the case now.
(1) The valuation v|H is a specialization of v.
Indeed, if|f|v|H≤|s|v| H̸= 0, then |s|v∈H and in particular |s|v̸= 0. Assume
|f|v >|s|v. Then |f|v /∈ H and hence |f|v < 1 because H contains cΓv. Thus
|s|v <|f|v < 1 and hence |f|v∈H because H is convex. Contradiction.
27
===== PDF PAGE 28 =====
We call v|H a primary or a horizontal specialization of v or v a horizontal gen-
eralization of v|H.
(2) One has supp( v)⊆ supp(v|H ) = ϕ−1(pA(v)p) with equality if and only if p = 0⇔
H = Γv.
(3) A(v)/p is a valuation ring of A(v)p/p, and v|H is the composition of the canonical
ring homomorphism π : A→ A(v)p/p followed by the valuation given by A(v)/p.
In other words, one has ker( π) = supp(v|H) and π induces an extension of valued
ﬁelds
(4.2.3) ( K(v|H ),A (v|H))֒→( A(v)p/p,A (v)/p).
Deﬁnition 4.17. Let A be a ring and let v be a valuation on A. A subset T of A is
called v-convex if t1,t 2∈T , s∈A, and v(t1)≤v(s)≤v(t2) imply s∈T .
A subsetT containing 0 isv-convex if and only if for t∈T ands∈A withv(s)≤v(t)
one has s∈T .
If v is a trivial valuation, the only v-convex sets T with 0 ∈ T are T = A and
T = suppv.
With this notion we can now describe the horizontal speciali zations of a given val-
uation.
Proposition 4.18. LetA be a ring and let v be a valuation on A. Let H be the set of
v-convex prime ideals, ordered by reversed inclusion, and le t S be the set of horizontal
specializations ofv, ordered by being a specialization. Then H andS are totally ordered
and the map
supp :S ={w ; w horizontal specialization of v}→ H ={v-convex prime ideal of A}
is a well-deﬁned isomorphism of totally ordered sets.
Proof. It is immediate that the support of a horizontal specializat ion of v is v-convex
and that supp: S→ H preserves the order. Moreover, every v-convex ideal clearly
contains suppv.
Letϕ : A→K := Frac(A/ supp(v)) be the canonical homomorphism. By deﬁnition,
the ordered set S is isomorphic to the set P of prime ideals p ofA(v) such that ϕ(A)⊆
A(v)p, endowed with the reversed inclusion order. As the set of ide als in a valuation
ring is totally ordered, P and hence S is totally ordered. Identifying S withP , the map
supp: S→H becomes p↦→ϕ−1(pAp). It is easy to check that an inverse map is given
by sending a v-convex prime ideal q to the ideal of A(v) generated by ϕ(q)∪A(v).
Our next goal is to show that every specialization is the hori zontal specialization of
a vertical specialization.
Lemma 4.19. Letw be a horizontal specialization of v.
(1) Letv′ a vertical specialization of v. Then there exists a unique vertical specialization
w′ of w such that w′ is a horizontal specialization of v′.
(2) Let w′ be a vertical specialization of w. Then there exists a vertical specialization
v′ of v such that w′ is a horizontal specialization of v′.

4.3 The valuation spectrum of a scheme
Let X be a scheme. A valuation of X is a pair ( x,v ), where x∈X and where v is the
equivalence class of a valuation on the residue ﬁeld of x. The value group of v is again
30
===== PDF PAGE 31 =====
denoted by Γ v. The set of valuation on X is denoted by Spv X. We endow it with the
topology generated by the sets of the form
{ (x,v )∈ SpvX ; x∈U,v (a(x))≤v(s(x))̸= 0},
where U⊆X is open and a,s∈ OX(U ).
Deﬁnition 4.22. The topological space Spv X is called valuation spectrum of X.
5 Topological algebra
5.1 Topological groups
Deﬁnition 5.1. A topological group is a set G endowed with the structure of a topo-
logical space and of a group such that the maps
G×G→G, (g,g′)↦→gg′, G →G, g ↦→g−1
are continuous (where we endow G×G with the product topology).
Remark 5.2. G topological group, a∈ G. Then left translation g↦→ag and right
tranlation g↦→ga are homeomorphisms G→ G. In particular the topology of G is
uniquely determined by a fundamental system of neighborhoo ds of one element of G.
The following trivial remark will be used very often.
Remark 5.3. Let G be a topological group, let H′⊆ H⊆ G be subgroups. If H′ is
open in G, then H is open in G.
Indeed, H = ⋃
h∈HhH′.
Proposition 5.4. G topological group, H ⊂ G subgroup. We endow G/H with the
quotient topology (i.e. if π : G→ G/H is the projection, U ⊆ G/H is deﬁned to be
open if and only if π−1(U ) is open).
(1) The closure
H is a subgroup of G.
(2) If H is locally closed in G, then H is closed in G.
(3) H is open in G (resp. closed in G) if and only if G/H is discrete (resp. Hausdorﬀ ).
(4) If H is a normal subgroup, G/H is a topological group.
Proof. Let a : G×G→G be the continuous map ( g,h )↦→gh−1. Then
a(H×H) = a(H×H)⊂a(H×H) = H.
This shows (1).
To prove (2) we may assume that H is open and dense in G (by replacing G by the
subgroup H). Then for g∈ G the two cosets gH and H have nonempty intersection
hence they are equal, i.e. g∈H.
(3): G/H discrete⇔ gH open in G for all g∈ G⇔ H open in G. If G/H is
Hausdorﬀ, then eH∈ G/H is a closed point and its inverse image H in G is closed.
Conversely, if H is closed, then H = HeH is a closed point in the quotient space
H\G/H. Hence its inverse image under the continuous map G/H×G/H→H\G/H,
(g1H,g 2H)↦→Hg−1
2 g1H is closed. But this is the diagonal of G/H×G/H.
(4) is clear.
31
===== PDF PAGE 32 =====
Corollary 5.5. G topological group,e∈G neutral element. Equivalent:
(i) G Hausdorﬀ.
(ii){e} is closed in G.
(iii){e} is the intersection of all its neighborhoods.
Proof. The equivalence of (i) and (ii) is a special case of Propositi on 5.4 (3). “(i) ⇒
(iii)” is obvious. Assume (iii) holds and let g̸=e. Then there exists a neighborhood V
of e such that g−1 /∈V , i.e. e /∈gV . Hence g is not in the closure of {e}.
The following example of a topological group will be essenti al.
Example 5.6. Let Γ be a non-empty partially ordered set which is right dire cted (i.e.,
for all γ,γ′∈ Γ there exists δ∈ Γ with δ≥ γ and δ≥ γ′). Let G be a group and let
(Gγ)γ∈Γ be a family of normal subgroups of G such thatGδ⊆Gγ ifδ≥γ. Equivalently,
let Γ be a set of normal subgroups such that for all Gγ,Gγ′∈ Γ there exists Gδ∈ Γ
such that Gδ⊆Gγ∩Gγ′ .
Then there exists on G a unique topology making G into a topological group such
that (Gγ)γ∈Γ is a fundamental system of neighborhoods of e (this uses that all Gγ are
normal). Moreover:
(1) By 5.4 the Gγ form a fundamental system of open and closed neighborhoods o f e.
(2) G is Hausdorﬀ if and only if ⋂
γGγ ={e}. In this case G is totally disconnected
by (1).
Very often we will encounter the following special case.

(1) We can endow G with the structure of a pseudometric space as follows. For g∈G

Remark 5.8. In general is a topological group G with unite metrisable if and only if G
is Hausdorﬀ and e has a countable fundamental system of neighborhoods ([BouT G] IX,
§3.1, Prop. 1). In this case there exist left-invariant metri cs (i.e. d(gh,gk ) = d(h,k )
for all g,h,k ∈G) and right-invarint metrics ([BouTG] IX, §3.1, Prop. 2).
Remark and Deﬁnition 5.9. Let f : G→ H be a continuous homomorphism of
topological groups. We endow G/ ker(f ) with the quotient topology induced by G and
f (G) with the subspace topology induced by H. Then f is called strict, if the following
equivalent conditions are satisﬁed.
(i) The bijective homomorphism G/ ker(f )→f (G) is a homeomorphism.
(ii) The induced homomorphism G→f (G) is open.
(iii) For all neighborhoods U of e in G its image f (U ) is a nieghborhood of e inf (G).
Remark 5.10. Let f : G→ H, g : H→ K be strict homomorphisms of topological
groups. If ker( g)⊆f (G), then it is not diﬃcult to show that the composition g◦f is
again strict.
But in general, g◦f is not strict, even if f is injective and g is surjective (e.g., let
x∈ R be an irrational number, let f be the inclusion Z֒→R and let g : R→ R/Z be
the canonical projection; use that Z + Zx is dense in R).
5.2 Topological rings
IfA is a ring and S,T ⊆ any subsets we denote by S·T the subgroup of (A, +) generated
by the elements st for s∈S and t∈T .
Deﬁnition 5.11. A topological ring is a set A endowed with the structure of a topo-
logical space and of a ring such that ( A, +) is a topological group and the map
A×A→A, (a,a′)↦→aa′
is continuous.
Let A be a topological ring. A topological A-module is an A-module E endowed
with a topology such that ( E, +) is a topological group and such that the scalar mul-
tiplication A×E→E is continuous.
Remark 5.12. Let A be a topological ring A.
(1) The homethety A→A, x↦→ax is continuous for all a∈A.
(2) The group of units A× with the subspace topology is in general not a topological
group (the map x↦→x−1 is not necessarily continuous). Instead we endow A×
usually with the ﬁnest topology such that the two maps A× → A, x↦→x and
x↦→x−1 are continuous. Then A× is a topological group.
Deﬁnition 5.13. A topological ﬁeld is a ﬁeld K together with a topology that makes
K into a topological ring and such that K×→ K×, x↦→x−1 is continuous (i.e. K×
with the subspace topology is a topological group).
33
===== PDF PAGE 34 =====
Remark 5.14. Let A be a topological ring and let I be an ideal. Similarly as in
Proposition 5.4 (1) one shows that its closure is also an idea l of A. In particular {0} is
an ideal of A.
Remark 5.15. Let K be a topological ﬁeld. As the closure of {0} is an ideal, it must
be either{0} orK. Therefore if K does not carry the chaotic topology (where the only
open subsets are ∅ and K), then K is Hausdorﬀ.

order is right directed (e.g. if Γ is a totally ordered abelia n group). Let A be a ring
and let (Aγ)γ∈Γ be a descending family of subgroups Aγ of (A, +) (descending means

We will also often encounter the following variant.
Example 5.17. Let Γ be a set of ideals of A such that for all Aγ,Aγ′∈ Γ there exists
Aδ ∈ Γ such that Aδ ⊆ Aγ∩Aγ′. Then there exists on A a unique topology that
makes A into a topological ring and such that the Aγ form a fundamental system of
neighborhoods of 0 in A ([BouTG] III, §6.3, Exemple 3). Such a topological ring A is
called linearly topologized and Γ is called a fundamental system of ideals .
An ideal I of A is then open if and only if it contains some ideal Aγ∈ Γ.
If (A, (Aγ )) is a linearly topologized ring, then an ideal I of A is called ideal of
deﬁnition if it is open and for all Aγ there exists an n> 0 such that In⊆Aγ. Note that
an ideal of deﬁnition does not necessarily exist. Even if it e xists, In is not necessarily
open.
A very important special case is the following.
Deﬁnition 5.18. A topological ring A is called adic if there exists an ideal I ofA such
that{In ; n≥ 0} is a fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0 in A. The ideal I is
then an ideal of deﬁnition and this topology is called the I-adic topology.
If E is an A-module, the topology deﬁned by ( InE)n is called the I-adic topology
on E.
34
===== PDF PAGE 35 =====
Warning: Here we deviate from the terminology in EGA, where adic topo logical rings
are always complete by deﬁnition.
The I-adic topology is also a special case of Example 5.16 with Γ = Z, An =A for
n≤ 0 and An =In for n> 0.
Remark 5.19. Let A be a ring and let I and J be ideals. Then the J-adic topology
is ﬁner than the I-adic topology if and only if there exists an integer n >0 such that
Jn⊆I.
Remark 5.20. Let A be a ring endowed with the I-adic topology for some ideal I of
A. Then A is Hausdorﬀ if and only if ⋂
n≥0In = 0. If A is noetherian, then by Krull’s
theorem ⋂
n≥0
In ={x∈A ; ∃a∈I : (1 + a)x = 0}
Thus the I-adic topology on a noetherian ring A is Hausdorﬀ if I is contained in the
Jacobson radical of A or if A has no zero divisors and I̸=A.
The following notion yields also a topological ring.
Deﬁnition 5.21. Let K be a ﬁeld endowed with a valuation |·| of height 1. After
replacing|·| by an equivalent valuation we may assume that |·| takes values in R≥0
(Proposition 1.14). A (non- archimedean) normed K-algebra is a unital K-algebra
A̸= 0 together with a map ||·|| : A→ R≥0, called norm, such that
(a) For x∈A one has||x|| = 0 if and only if x = 0.
(b)||x +y||≤ max{||x||,||y||} for all x,y∈A.
(c)||λx|| =|λ|||x|| for all x∈A, λ∈K.
(d)||xy||≤||x||||y|| for all x,y∈A.
(e)||1||≤ 1.
As usual the norm yields on A the structure of a metric space and in particular that
of a topological space. The properties of ||·|| ensure that A together with this topology
is a topological ring.
Of course, the notion of a normed algebra is also used over ﬁel ds with an archimedean
absolute value. In this case one replaces condition (b) by th e usual triangle inequation.
Remark 5.22. Let (A,||·|| ) be a normed algebra over a real-valued ﬁeld ( K,|·| ) with
A̸= 0.
(1) Condition (a) implies that ||1||̸= 0 and condition (d) implies ||1||≤|| 1||2 and hence
||1|| = 1 because of condition (e). Thus K→A, λ↦→λ1 is an isometry.
(2) A0 :={a∈ A ; ||a||≤ 1} is an open subring of A. It is an algebra over the open
subring OK :={λ∈K ; |λ|≤ 1} of K.
All of the above examples are a special case of the following t ype of ring.
Deﬁnition 5.23. A topological ring A is called non-archimedean ifA has a fundamen-
tal system of neighborhoods of 0 consisting of subgroups of ( A, +).
35
===== PDF PAGE 36 =====
Remark 5.24. LetA be a ring and let G be a set of subgroups of ( A, +). Then G is a
fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0 of a (unique) topol ogy that makes A into a
topological ring if and only if G satisﬁes the following properties.
(a) For all G,G′∈G there exists H∈G with H⊆G∩G′.
(b) For all x∈A and all G∈G there exists H∈G with xH⊆G.
(c) For all G∈G there exists H∈G with H·H⊆G.
5.3 Bounded sets and topologically nilpotent elements
For every subset T of a ring and for n∈ N we set
(5.3.1) T (n) :={t1t2··· tn ; ti∈T}.
Deﬁnition 5.25. Let A be a topological ring. An element x∈ A is called topolog-
ically nilpotent if 0 is a limit of the sequence ( xn)n∈N. We denote by Aoo the set of
topologically nilpotent elements of A.
More generally, we call a subset T of A topologically nilpotent if there exists for
every neighborhood U of 0 an N∈ N such that T (n)⊆U for all n≥N .
Clearly x∈A is topologically nilpotent if and only if {x} is topologically nilpotent.
Remark 5.26. Let A be a linearly topologized ring. Then x∈ A is topologically
nilpotent if and only if for every open ideal J of A the image of x in A/J is nilpotent.
Therefore the set Aoo of topologically nilpotent elements is an ideal of A.
Assume now that A has an ideal of deﬁnition I. Then the following assertions are
equivalent for x∈A.
(i) x is topologically nilpotent.
(ii) The image in A/I is nilpotent.
(iii) x is contained in an ideal of deﬁnition.
Indeed, clearly one has “(iii) ⇒ (i)⇒ (ii)”. Let x∈A with xm∈I. Then I +Ax is
open and (I +Ax)m⊆I. Thus I +Ax is an ideal of deﬁnition.
In particular we see that Aoo is the inverse image of the nilradical of A/I and hence
it is an open ideal of A. Moreover, Aoo is itself an ideal of deﬁnition (and then clearly
the largest ideal of deﬁnition) if and only if the nilradical of A/I is nilpotent. This is
automatic if the nilradical of A/I is ﬁnitely generated (e.g., if A/I is noetherian).
Deﬁnition 5.27. LetA be a topological ring. A subset B ofA is called bounded if for
every neighborhood U of 0 in A there exists an open neighborhood V of 0 in A such
that vb∈U for all v∈V and b∈B.
An element x∈A is called power-bounded if the set {xn ; n≥ 1} is bounded. The
set of power- bounded elements is denoted by A0.
More generally, a subset T of A is called power-bounded if ⋃
n∈NT (n) is bounded.
Remark 5.28. Let A be a topological ring.
(1) Clearly, every ﬁnite subset of A is bounded.
(2) Every subset of a (power-)bounded subset is (power-)bou nded.
36
===== PDF PAGE 37 =====
(3) Every ﬁnite union of (power-)bounded subsets is (power- )bounded. A ﬁnite union
of bounded and topologically nilpotent subsets is again top ologically nilpotent.
(4) A bounded and topologically nilpotent subset of A is power-bounded.
(5) If T1 is a power-bounded subset of A and T2 a topologically nilpotent subset of A.
Then{t1t2 ; t1∈T1,t 2∈T2} is topologically nilpotent.
Example 5.29. (1) Let A = C. Then a subset B of C is bounded if and only if there
exists C >0 such that |z|<C for all z∈ C. One has Ao ={z∈ C ; |z|≤ 1} and
Aoo ={z∈ C ; |z|< 1}.
(2) Let A be a ring and |·| a non-trivial valuation on A.
Assume that|·| takes values in R>∪{ 0} (i.e.|·| is of height 1). Endow A with
the structure of a topological ring such that the subgroups {x∈A ; |x| < ε} for
ε∈ R>0 form a fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0 in A (Example 5.16).
Then again a subset B ofA of set is bounded if and only if there exists C >0 such
that|x| < C for all x∈ A. One has Ao ={x∈ A ; |x|≤ 1} and Aoo ={x∈
A ; |x|< 1}.
If|·| has height≥ 2, then there exists a non-trivial convex subgroup ∆ of Γ |·|.
If x∈A is an element with |x|∈ ∆ then x is never topologically nilpotent even if
|x|< 1.
Proposition 5.30. LetA be a non-archimedean topological ring.
(1) Let T be a subset of A and let T′ be the subgroup of A generated by T . Then T′
is bounded (resp. power-bounded, resp. topologcially nilp otent) if and only if T has
this property.
(2) A subset T of A is power-bounded if and only if the subring of A generated by T is
bounded.
(3) The union of all bounded subrings is Ao, and this is a subring of A.
(4) Ao is integrally closed in A, and Aoo is a radical ideal of Ao.
Proof. (1) follows from the deﬁnitions. To show (2), we may assume th at 1∈T . Then
the subring generated by T is the subgroup of A generated by X := ⋃
n∈NT (n). As X
is bounded, (2) follows from (1).
IfA1 andA2 are bounded subrings, then by (1) the subring generated by A1∪A2 is
bounded. Thus the union B of all bounded subrings is a subring. Now it follows from
(2) that B =Ao.
It remains to show (4). Let a∈ A be integral over Ao. By (3), a is integral over
a bounded subring B of A. Therefore there exists an integer N such that B[a] =
B +Ba +··· +BaN−1. Thus B[a] is bounded and hence a is power-bounded. For
a∈Aoo the set{a} is bounded and topologically nilpotent, hence it is power-b ounded.
Thus Aoo⊆ Ao. By (1) it is a subgroup of Ao and by Remark 5.28 (5) it is an ideal
of Ao. Let a∈ A such that am∈ Aoo. We want to show that a∈ Aoo. Let U be
a neighborhood of 0 in A. Choose a neighborhood V of 0 such that aiv⊆ U for all
i = 0,...,m − 1 and all v∈ V . Choose N∈ N such that ( am)n∈ V for all n≥ N .
Then an∈U for all n≥mN .

===== PDF PAGE 38 =====
5.4 Completion of abelian topological groups and of topolog ical rings
Deﬁnition 5.31. (1) Let X be a set. A ﬁlter basis on X is a non-empty set B of
subsets of X such that ∅ /∈B and such that for all A1,A 2∈B there exists A∈B
with A⊆A1∩A2.
Example: Let ( xn)n be a sequence in X. Then {{xk ; k≥ n} ; n∈ N} is a
ﬁlter basis on X, called the ﬁlter basis of (xn).
(2) Let X be a topological space, B a ﬁlter basis on X, and x∈X. Then B converges
to x if for every open neighborhood U of x there exists A∈B such that A⊆U .
Example: The ﬁlter basis of a sequence ( xn) converges to x if and only if ( xn)
converges to x.
(3) Let G be an abelian topological group (written additively). A ﬁlt er basis B is
called Cauchy if for every open neighborhood U of 0 there exists A∈B such that
x−y∈U for all x,y∈A.
(4) An abelian topological group G is called complete if G is Hausdorﬀ and every
Cauchy ﬁlter basis converges.
(5) A topological ring A is called complete if (A, +) is a complete topological group.
Warning: Here we deviate from Bourbaki’s terminology, where comple te topological
groups are not necessarily Hausdorﬀ.
Proposition and Deﬁnition 5.32. LetG be an abelian topological group (resp. topo-
logical ring). Then there exists a complete abelian topologi cal group ˆG (resp. complete
topological ring) and a continuous homomorphism ι : G→ ˆG satisfying the following
universal property. For every complete abelian topologica l group (resp. complete topo-
logical ring) H and every continuous homomorphism ϕ : G→H there exists a unique
continuous homomorphism ˆϕ : ˆG→H such that ˆϕ◦ι =ϕ. Clearly ( ˆG,ι ) is unique up
to unique isomorphism. Moreover:
(1) ker(ι) =
{0} =:N , im(ι) is dense in ˆG.
(2) IfU is a basis of neighborhoods of 0 in G, then {ι(U ) closure in ˆG ; U∈U} is a
basis of neighborhoods of 0 in ˆG.
(3) The formation of ˆG is functorial in G.
Proof. [BouTG] III, §3.4.
Example 5.33. LetG be an abelian topological group, where the topology is given by
a family (Gγ) of open subgroups of G as in Example 5.6. Then G/Gγ is discrete and the
canonical homomorphismG→G/Gγ yields by the universal property of the completion

(2) The ˆGγ form a fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0 in ˆG and hence are open
subgroups of ˆG.

Proof. The equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows from the fact that G is metrizable.
(ii)⇒ (iii): For every n≥ 1 there exists M≥ 1 such that xm∈Gn for all m≥M .

the series.
(iii)⇒ (ii): Standard exercise in non-archimedean geometry.
The equivalence of (i), (iv), and (v) is [BouTG] III, §7.3.

Proof. [BouTG] IX,§3.1, Prop. 4.

Proposition 5.38. LetA be a complete non-archimedean ring. Let a⊆A be an ideal
such that every element of a is topologically nilpotent. Then an element x of A is
invertible if and only if its image in A/a is invertible. In particular a is contained in
the Jacobson radical of A.

5.5 The topology deﬁned by a valuation
Proposition and Deﬁnition 5.39. Let A be a ring and let v be a valution on A.
For γ∈ Γv we set Aγ :={a∈ A ; v(a) < γ}. Then (Aγ)γ satisﬁes the conditions
of Example 5.16 and ⋃
γAγ = A. Thus A becomes a topologcial ring with (Aγ) as a
fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0. This topology is called the topology deﬁned
byv. Moreover:
(1) If suppv = 0, then A is Hausdorﬀ and totally disconnected.
(2) The group of units A× endowed with the subspace topology is a topological group.
Proof. One has ⋂
γAγ = suppv. This shows (1). To show (2) we have to prove that
A×→A×, x↦→x−1 is continuous. Let x0∈A×. It suﬃces to show that for all γ∈ Γv
and x∈A× one has
|x−x0|v < min{γ|x0|2

But x−1−x−1

0 |v =|x−x0|v|x|−1

v . If
|x−x0|v <|x0|v, one has |x|v =|x0|v. If |x−x0|v <γ|x0|2

Corollary 5.40. Letv be a valuation on a ﬁeld K. Then the topology deﬁned by v on
K makes K into a topological ﬁeld.
Remark 5.41. Letv be a non-trivial valuation on a ring A with value group Γ. Then
{a∈A ; v(a)≤γ},{a∈A ; v(a)≥γ},{a∈A ; v(a)>γ } are open and closed in
the topology deﬁned by v.
Indeed, by deﬁnition {a∈ A ; v(a) < γ} is an open subgroup of the subgroup
{a∈A ; v(a)≤γ}. Thus {a∈A ; v(a)≤γ} is open and (being a subgroup) closed.
The other sets are open and closed by taking complements.
Remark 5.42. Let K be a ﬁeld and let v be a valuation on K with value group Γ.
Endow Γ with the discrete topology. Let ˆK the completion ring. This is a topological
ﬁeld ([BouAC] VI, §5.3, Prop. 5).
The homomorphism v : K×→ Γ is continuous and thus can be extended uniquely
to a continuous homomorphism ˆv : ˆK×→ Γ and by continuity ˆv deﬁnes a valuation on
ˆK. For γ∈ Γ the closures of {x∈ K ; v(x) < γ} and{x∈ K ; v(x)≤ γ} in ˆK
are{x∈ ˆK ; ˆv(x) < γ} and{x∈ ˆK ; ˆv(x)≤ γ}, respectively. In particular, the
topology on ˆK is deﬁned by the valuation ˆ v. Moreover, one obtains for its valuation
ring A(ˆv) = ˆA(v) and for its maximal ideal m(ˆv) = ˆm(v).
MoreoverA(ˆv) = A + m(ˆv) and in particular κ(ˆv) = κ(v).
Deﬁnition 5.43. Two valuations v1 and v2 on a ﬁeld K are called independent if K
is the ring generated by A(v1) and A(v2). Otherwise they are called dependent.
The trivial valuation is independent of every other valuati on. Clearly two equivalent
non-trivial valuations are dependent. More generally, if A(v1)⊆ A(v2)̸= K, then v1
and v2 are dependent.
40
===== PDF PAGE 41 =====
Proposition 5.44. Two valuations of height 1 are equivalent if and only if they are
dependent.
Proof. [BouAC] VI,§4.5, Prop. 6 (c).
There are examples of dependent non-equivalent valuations which have both height
2 ([BouAC] VI, §8, Exercise 1).
Proposition 5.45. Two non-trivial valuations v1 and v2 on a ﬁeld K are dependent
if and only if they deﬁne the same topology on K.
Proof. [BouAC] VI,§7.2, Prop. 3
Remark and Deﬁnition 5.46. Let v be a valuation on a ﬁeld K and let A its
valuation ring. Then v (or A) is called microbial if the following equivalent assertions
are satisﬁed.
(i) There exists a height 1 valuation which is dependent to v.
(ii) There exists exists a topologically nilpotent element ̸= 0 in K.
(iii) A equipped with the valuation topology is non-discrete and ad ic.
(iv) A has a prime ideal of height 1.
(v) There exists a convex subgroup H of Γ v such that Γ v/H has height 1.
(vi) There exists a non-trivial homomorphisms of totally or dered groups Γ v→ R.
Every valuation of ﬁnite height ̸= 0 is microbial.

5.7 Tate algebras
In this subsection we let k be a ﬁeld which is complete with respect to a valuation
|·| : k→ R≥0. Its valuation ring is then Henselian (Remark 2.19), and we e ndow any
algebraic extension k′ of k with the unique extension of |·| to k′ (Proposition 2.18).
This extension is again called |·| .

Lemma 5.55. Let ¯k be an algebraic closure of k and let
Bn(¯k) :={ (x1,...,x n)∈ ¯kn ; |xi|≤ 1 for all i = 1,...,n }
be the unit ball in ¯kn. Then a formal power series f = ∑

Lemma 5.56. Tn is noetherian.
Proof. [BGR] 6.1.1 Proposition 3.
Deﬁnition 5.57. A topological k-algebraA is called topologically of ﬁnite type over k
if there exists a continuous open surjective k-algebra homomorphism Tn→A.
Thus every k-algebraA topologically of ﬁnite type is of the form Tn/a, where a is a
closed ideal, and where the topology on A is the quotient topology of the topology on
Tn. Conversely:
Proposition 5.58. Let a be an ideal of Tn. Let π : Tn→ Tn/a be the canonical pro-
jection. Then a is closed in Tn. Endow the k-algebra A := Tn/a with the quotient
topology. Then A is a complete k-algebra and its topology is induced by the norm
|a|π := inf{|f| ; f∈Tn with π(f ) = a}.
The canonical projection π is open and contractive.
Proof. If A is any commutative noetherian Banach k-algebra, then any ﬁnitely gener-
ated ideal is closed and A/a is complete. Thus a is closed in Tn and Tn/a is complete.
Then clearly π is continuous. It is not diﬃcult to check that |·| π is a norm, and it is
immediate that π is contractive. Further by Banach’s theorem, any continuou sk-linear
surjective map of Banach spaces is open. In particular π is open.
Lemma 5.56 implies:
Proposition 5.59. Every k-algebra topologically of ﬁnite type is noetherian.

Proposition 5.60. Everyk-algebra homomorphism between k-algebras topologically of
ﬁnite type is continuous.
Proof. [BGR] 6.1.3 Theorem 1.
Proposition 5.61. Let A be a k-algebra topologically of ﬁnite type. Then for every
maximal ideal m the quotient A/m is a ﬁnite extension of k.
6 f -adic rings and Tate rings
6.1 f -adic rings
Proposition and Deﬁnition 6.1. A topological ringA is called f -adic if the following
equivalent assertions are satisﬁed.
(i) There exists a subset U of A and a ﬁnite subset T⊆U such that {Un ; n≥ 1} is
a fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0 in A and such that T·U =U 2⊆U .
(ii) A contains on open subring A0 such that the subspace topology on A0 is I-adic,
whereI is a ﬁnitely generated ideal of A0.
A subring A0 (resp. a pair (A0,I )) as in (ii) is called a ring of deﬁnition (resp. a pair
of deﬁnition ).
Clearly any f -adic ring is non-archimedean.
Proof. We prove the equivalence together with the following lemma.
Lemma 6.2. LetA be an f -adic ring. Then for a subring A0 of A (endowed with the
subspace topology) the following assertions are equivalen t.
(a) A0 is a ring of deﬁnition.
(b) A0 is open in A and adic.
(c) A0 is open in A and bounded.
Proof. Let A be a topological ring. We ﬁrst remark that every open adic sub ring
clearly is bounded. In particular (b) implies (c) for an arbi trary topological ring. The
implication “(a)⇒ (b)” is trivial.
IfA satisﬁes (ii) in Proposition 6.1, then we set U :=I and let T be a ﬁnite system
of generators of I and see that A satisﬁes (i).
From now on let us assume that there exists a subset U of A and a ﬁnite subset
T⊆ U such that {Un ; n≥ 1} is a fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0 in A
and such that T·U = U 2⊆ U . Let W be the subgroup of ( A, +) generated by U .
Then W is open because U is open. As Un⊆U , Z· 1 +W is an open subring of A. It
is also a bounded (Remark 5.30).
Thus we have shown all equivalences if we can prove that (c) im plies (a). Let A0 be
an open and bounded subring of A. For every n∈ N putT (n) ={t1t2··· tn ; ti∈T}.
Choose k∈ N such that T (k)⊆A0 and let I be the ideal of A0 generated by T (k). Let
l∈ N such that Ul⊆A0. Then we have for all n∈ N
In =T (nk)A0⊇T (nk)Ul =Ul+nk.
46
===== PDF PAGE 47 =====
Thus In is an open neighborhood of 0. Let V be any neighborhood of 0. As A0 is
bounded, there exists m∈ N such that UmA0⊆V . Then Im⊆V . Therefore ( In)n∈N
is a fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0.
Remark 6.3. The topology of an f -adic ring A is metrisable (Remark 5.7). Thus
Proposition 5.35 shows that if A is complete and a⊆A is a closed ideal, then A/a is
complete.
Corollary 6.4. LetA be an f -adic ring.
(1) IfA0 andA1 are rings of deﬁnitions, then A0∩A1 andA0·A1 are rings of deﬁnitions.
(2) Every open subring C of A is f -adic.
(3) Ao is a subring of A and it is the union of all rings of deﬁnitions of A.
(4) Let B be a bounded subring of A and let C be an open subring of A with B⊆ C.
Then there exists a ring of deﬁnition A0 with B⊆A0⊆C.
(5) A is adic if and only if it is bounded.
Proof. Assertions (1) is clear, because if A0 andA1 are open and bounded, then A0∩A1
and A0·A1 are open and bounded. Assertion (2) is obvious, and (3) follo ws from
Proposition 5.30 (3). To show (4) one may assume A =C by (2) and if A0 is any ring
of deﬁnition, then A0·B is a ring of deﬁnition containing B. Finally Assertion (5)
follows immeditately from Lemma 6.2.
Example 6.5. (1) Every adic ring with a ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁniti on isf -adic.
(2) Let k be a ﬁeld endowed with the topolopgy deﬁned by a valuation v. Then k is
f -adic if and only if v is trivial or v is microbial.
(3) A ﬁeld k whose topology is deﬁned by a microbial valuation v is called a non-
archimedean ﬁeld . Then the topology is also given by a nontrivial valuation |·|
with values in R≥0. Then ko ={x∈ k ; |x|≤ 1} and koo =: m is its maximal
ideal. The subring ko is open in k and it is an I-adic ring, where I is an ideal
generated by some element π with|π|< 1.
If|·| is a discrete valuation, then we may take for I the maximal ideal. But in
generalko does not carry the m-adic topology (e.g., if Γ |·|∼
= Q, then mn = m for all
n≥ 1).
The valuation ring of v is contained in ko.
(4) Let k be a non-archimedean ﬁeld and let Tn :=k⟨X1,...,X n⟩ be the ring of con-
vergent power series. Then Tn is an f -adic ring. For the ring of power-bounded
elements we have

and To
