Lemma 6.6. Let A be an f -adic ring and let n be the ideal of A generated by the
topologically nilpotent elements of A. Then an ideal a of A is open if and only if
n⊆ rad(a).
Proof. If a is open, then for every topologically nilpotent element f∈ A there exists
n∈ N such that fn∈ a, hence n⊆ rad(a). Conversely, assume that n⊆ rad(a). Let A0
be a ring of deﬁnition of A and let I be a ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁnition of A0.
Then I⊆ n⊆ rad(a) and thus there exists some power Im contained in a. But Im is
open again and therefore a is open.
In particular one has for an f -adic ring A and all ideals of deﬁnition I of a ring of
deﬁnition
{ p∈ SpecA ; p open in A} =V (Aoo) = V (I),
i.e., a prime ideal is open if and only if it contains every ide al of deﬁnition of every ring
of deﬁnition of A.
Remark 6.7. Let A be an f -adic ring, let B be a topological ring and let π : A→B
be a continuous surjective open homomorphism. Then B is f -adic.
Remark 6.8. Let A be a f -adic ring, B a ring of deﬁnition of A, and I a ﬁnitely
generated ideal of deﬁnition of B. We consider the completion ˆB as an open subring
of ˆA. By Proposition 5.37, ˆB is adic, and I ˆB is an ideal of deﬁnition of ˆB. Hence ˆA is
an f -adic ring.
Proposition 6.9. LetA be a f -adic ring, B a ring of deﬁnition of A.
(1) The canonical ring homomorphism A⊗B ˆB→ ˆA is an isomorphism.
(2) If B is noetherian, then A→ ˆA is ﬂat.
(3) If A is a ﬁnitely generated B-algebra and B is noetherian, then ˆA is noetherian.
Proof. Assertion (1) is [Hu2] Lemma 1.6. The remaining assertions f ollow immediately.
6.2 Tate rings
Deﬁnition 6.10. A topological ring is called Tate ring if it is f -adic and has a topo-
logically nilpotent unit.
Remark 6.11. An adic ring A is a Tate ring if and only if its topology is the chaotic
topology.
Example 6.12. Let k be a ﬁeld endowed with a non-trivial valuation v. Then k with
the valuation topology is a Tate ring if and only if v is microbial (Deﬁnition 5.46).
Then A(v) is a ring of deﬁnition.
48
===== PDF PAGE 49 =====
Example 6.13. Let k be a ﬁeld endowed with a non-trivial valuation |·| . Assume
that|·| takes values in R≥0. Every normed k-algebra (A,||·|| ) is a Tate ring: A0 :=
{a∈A ;||a||≤ 1} is an open subring and if r∈k× is any element with v(r)< 1, then
(rnA0)n∈N is a fundamental system of neighborhoods of 0 in A0.
In particular every algebra topologically of ﬁnite type ove r k (Example 5.54) is a
Tate ring.
Proposition 6.14. Let A be a Tate ring and let B be a ring of deﬁnition. Then B
contains a topologically nilpotent unit s of A. For any such s one has A =Bs and sB
is an ideal of deﬁnition of B.
Proof. AsB is an open neighborhood of 0 there exists for every topologic ally nilpotent
elementt an n∈ N such that tn∈B. This shows the ﬁrst assertion.
For everya∈A there exists n∈ N such thatasn∈B, hence A =Bs. Multiplication
with sn is a homeomorphism A→A. This shows that snB is open. Moreover, as B is
bounded, for every neighborhood V of 0 there exists n∈ N such that snB⊆V .
Corollary 6.15. Letk be a topological ﬁeld whose topology is given by a valuation o f
height 1. Let π∈ k× be a topologically nilpotent element and let A be a topological
k-algebra. Then A is a Tate ring if and only if it contains a subring A0 such that
(πnA0)n is a fundamental system of open neighborhoods of 0 of A.
Proof. The condition is necessary by Proposition 6.14. Conversely , if A0 is a subring
such that (πnA0)n is a fundamental system of open neighborhoods of 0 of A, then A0
is an open subring and as linearly topologized ring it is boun ded. Thus A0 is a ring of
deﬁnition.
6.3 Banach’s theorem for Tate rings
Recall the following version of Banach’s theorem:
Theorem 6.16. Let A be a topological ring that has a sequence converging to 0 con-
sisting of units of A (e.g., if A is a Tate ring). Let M and N be Hausdorﬀ topological
A-modules that have countable fundamental systems of open ne ighborhoods of 0. As-
sume that M is complete. Let u : M→N be an A-linear map. Consider the following
properties.
(a) N is complete.
(b) u is surjective.
(c) u is open.
Then any two of these properties imply the third.
The proof will show that u is already open and surjective if N is complete and u(M )
is open in N .
Proof. Missing

A-module that has a countable fundamental system of open neig hborhoods of 0. Then
M is noetherian if and only if every submodule of M is closed. In particular A is
noetherian if and only if every ideal is closed.
Proof. Missing
Proposition 6.18. LetA be a complete noetherian Tate ring.
(1) Every ﬁnitely generated A-module has a unique A-module topology that is complete
and that has a countable fundamental system of open neighbor hoods of 0.
(2) Letf : M→N be an A-linear map of ﬁnitely generated modules that are endowed
with the topology from (1). Then f is continuous and the map f : M→ f (M ) is

Remark 6.19. Let A be a complete noetherian Tate ring, A0 a ring of deﬁnition and
s∈ A0 a topologically nilpotent unit of A (such that A0 has the sA0-adic topology).
LetM be a ﬁnitely generated A-module and choose a ﬁnitely generated A0-submodule

6.4 Examples
Lemma 6.20. Let A be an f -adic ring and let T be a subset such that ideal of A
generated by T is open in A. Then Tn·U is a neighborhood of zero in A for all n∈ N
and for all open subgroups U of A.
Proof. Let U and n be given. As T·A is a neighborhood of zero, Tn·A = (T·A)n
is a neighborhood of zero. Let A0 be a ring of deﬁnition and I an ideal of deﬁnition
of A0 such that I⊆ Tn·A. Let L be a ﬁnite system of generators of I and let K be
a ﬁnite subset of A such that L⊆ Tn·K. Choose m∈ N with K·Im⊆ U . Then
Im+1 =L·Im⊆ (Tn·K)·Im =Tn· (K·Im)⊆Tn·U .
Proposition 6.21. Let A be an f -adic ring, let B be a ring of deﬁnition and let I a
ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁnition of B. Let Λ be a set, and let T = (Tλ)λ∈Λ be a
family of subsets of A such that the ideal of A generated byTλ is open for all λ.
(1) Then A[X]T is an f -adic ring, B[X] is a ring of deﬁnition, and I[X] =I·B[X] is a
ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁnition. If A is a Tate ring, then A[X]T is a Tate ring.
(2) Assume that Λ is ﬁnite. Then A⟨X⟩T is an f -adic ring, B⟨X⟩ is a ring of deﬁnition,
and I⟨X⟩ = I·B⟨X⟩ is a ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁnition. If A is a Tate ring,
then A⟨X⟩T is a Tate ring.
(3) LetS ={sλ ; λ∈ Λ} be a family of elements of A. Then the topological localiza-
tions A(T

S⟩ are f -adic rings. If A is a Tate ring, then A(T
S ) and A⟨T
S⟩
are also Tate rings.

By Lemma 6.20 the topological rings A[X]T , A⟨X⟩T , A(T

Assertion (2) also holds for arbitrary index sets Λ if one ass umes in addition that A
is Hausdorﬀ and that Tλ is bounded for all λ∈ Λ.
Proof. The inclusions I[X] = I·B[X] and I·B⟨X⟩⊆ I⟨X⟩ are clear. Let us show that

Clearly the rings B[X] and B⟨X⟩ are open subrings of A[X] and A⟨X⟩, respectively.
Moreover, if I is an ideal of deﬁnition of B, then Im is also an ideal of deﬁnition of B.
Thus we have shown that ( Im)[X] = Im·B[X] and (Im)⟨X⟩ = Im·B⟨X⟩. This shows
that B[X] and B⟨X⟩ are adic rings. Thus we have seen that A[X] and A⟨X⟩ are f -adic
rings.

Remark 6.22. LetA be an adic ring. Then A⟨X⟩T is in general not adic. In fact one

6.5 Adic homomorphisms
Deﬁnition 6.23. Let A and B be f -adic rings. A ring homomorphism ϕ : A→ B
is called adic if there exist rings of deﬁnitions A0 of A and B0 of B and an ideal of
deﬁnition I of A0 such that ϕ(A0)⊆B0 and such that ϕ(I)B0 is an ideal of deﬁnition
of B0.
Any adic ring homomorphism is continuous. Conversely, for e very continuous ho-
momorphism ϕ : A→ B there exist always rings of deﬁnitions A0 of A and B0 of B
and ﬁnitely generated ideals of deﬁnition I of A0 and J of B0 such that ϕ(A0)⊆ B0
and such that ϕ(I)⊆J. But in general ϕ(I)B0 is not an ideal of deﬁnition of B0.
Example 6.24. LetA be a discrete ring. Then A is adic with ideal of deﬁnition I = 0.
Any homomorphism ϕ : A→B to an f -adic ring B is continuous. It is adic if and only
if B also carries the discrete topology.
Proposition 6.25. Letϕ : A→B be a continuous ring homomorphism between f -adic
rings. Assume that A is a Tate ring. Then B is a Tate ring, ϕ is adic, and for every
ring of deﬁnition B0 of B one has ϕ(A)·B0 =B.
51
===== PDF PAGE 52 =====
Proof. Let A0 (resp. B0) be rings of deﬁnition of A (resp. B) such that ϕ(A0)⊆ B0.
Lets∈A0 be a topologically nilpotent unit of A. Then ϕ(s) is a topologically nilpotent
unit of B and hence B is a Tate ring. Replacing s be some power, we may assume that
s∈A0. Then sA0 is an ideal of deﬁnition of A0 and ϕ(s)B0 is an ideal of deﬁnition of
B0 (Proposition 6.14). This shows that ϕ is adic. Let B0⊆B be an arbitrary ring of
deﬁnition. Replacing s by some power we may assuume that ϕ(s)∈B0. Moreover one
has A = (A0)s and B = (B0)ϕ(s) by Proposition 6.14 and hence ϕ(A)·B0 =B.
Proposition 6.26. Letϕ : A→B and ψ : B→C be continuous ring homomorphisms
of f -adic rings.
(1) If ϕ and ψ are adic, then ψ◦ϕ is adic.
(2) If ψ◦ϕ is adic, then ψ is adic.
Proof. [Hu2] Corollary 1.9
Remark 6.27. Let ϕ : A→ B be a continuous ring homomorphisms of f -adic rings.
Let A′⊆ A and B′⊆ B be open subrings (then A′ and B′ are again f -adic rings by
Corollary 6.4) such that ϕ(A′)⊆B′. Then if ϕ is adic, its restriction A′→B′ is also
adic.
6.6 Homomorphisms topologically of ﬁnite type
Deﬁnition 6.28. Let A be an f -adic ring. A ring homomorphism ϕ : A→ B to a
completef -adic ring B is called strictly topologically of ﬁnite type if there exist n∈ N0
and a surjective continuous open ring homomorphism
π : ˆA⟨X1,...,X n⟩ ։B
of A-algebras.
Proposition and Deﬁnition 6.29. LetA be an f -adic ring. A ring homomorphism
ϕ : A→ B to a complete f -adic ring B is called topologically of ﬁnite type if the
following equivalent conditions are satisﬁed.
(i) There exist n∈ N0, ﬁnite subsets T1,...,T n of A such that Ti·A is open in A for
all i = 1,...,n , and a surjective continuous open ring homomorphism
π : ˆA⟨X1,...,X n⟩T1,...,Tn ։B
of A-algebras.
(ii) The homomorphism ϕ is adic, there exists a ﬁnite subset M of B such that A[M ]
is dense in B, and there exist rings of deﬁnition A0⊆A and B0⊆B and a ﬁnite
subset N of B0 such that ϕ(A0)⊆B0 and such that A0[N ] is dense in B0.
(iii) There exist rings of deﬁnition A0⊆A and B0⊆B with ϕ(A0)⊆B0 such that B0
is an A0-algebra strictly topologically of ﬁnite type such and such that B is ﬁnitely
generated over A·B0.
(iv) For every open subring A0 ofA there exists an open subring B0 ofB withϕ(A0)⊆
B0 such that B0 is an A0-algebra strictly topologically of ﬁnite type and such that
B is ﬁnitely generated over A·B0.

the existence of a continuous surjective open homomorphism A0⟨X1,...,X m⟩→ B0
implies that B0 is adic.
Proof. “(i) ⇒ (iii)”. Let (A0,I ) be a pair of deﬁnition of A. Then ˆA⟨X⟩T is the

ˆ(A0)[X]. As A[X]T is of ﬁnite type over A· (A0)[X], this
implies the claim.
“(iii)⇒ (iv)”. [Hu1] 2.3.25
“(iv) ⇒ (ii) ⇒ (iii)”. The fact that (iv) implies that ϕ is adic follows from
Proposition 6.21. All other implications are clear.
“(iv)⇒ (i)”. We may assume that A is complete. By hypothesis ϕ factors through
a continuous open homomorphism σ : A⟨X1,...,X n⟩→ B such that B is generated
as A⟨X1,...,X n⟩-algebra by ﬁnitely many elements b1,...,b s. Let A0 be a ring of
deﬁnition of A and let L ⊆ A0 a subset which generates an ideal of deﬁnition of
A0. Choose k ∈ N such that Ikbj is power-bounded for all j = 1,...,s . We set
T := (T1,...,T m+s) with Ti :={1} fori = 1,...,m andTi =Ik fori =m + 1,...,m +
s. Then the universal property of A⟨X1,...,X m+s⟩T (Corollary 5.50) implies that
there exists a continuous homomorphism of A-algebras ρ : A⟨X1,...,X m+s⟩T → B
such that ρ(Xi) = σ(Xi) for i = 1,...,m and ρ(Xj+m) = bj for j = 1,...,s . Then ρ
is surjective. Moreover, for every open subgroup U of A the image of U⟨X1,...,Xm+s⟩⊆
A⟨X1,...,X m+s⟩T under ρ contains the image of U⟨X1,...,Xm⟩⊆ A⟨X1,...,X m⟩ under
σ. Therefore ρ is open because σ is open.
The same argument also shows the following strict variant.
Proposition 6.31. LetA be an f -adic ring. Let ϕ : A→B be a ring homomorphism
to a complete f -adic ring B. Then the following conditions are equivalent.
(i) ϕ is strictly topologically of ﬁnite type.
(ii) There exist rings of deﬁnition A0⊆A and B0⊆B with ϕ(A0)⊆B0 such that B0
is an A0-algebra strictly topologically of ﬁnite type such and such that B =A·B0.
(iii) For every open subring A0 ofA there exists an open subring B0 ofB withϕ(A0)⊆
B0 such that B0 is an A0-algebra strictly topologically of ﬁnite type and such that
B =A·B0.
Example 6.32. (1) A ring homomorphism between discrete topological rings is topo-
logically of ﬁnite type if and only if it is of ﬁnite type.
(2) Let A be an f -adic ring, s1,...,s n∈A andTi⊆A ﬁnite subsets such that Ti·A is
open in A for i = 1,...,n . Then the canonical homomorphism A→A⟨T1

is topologically of ﬁnite type.
Indeed, this follows from Proposition 6.29 (ii) by taking (w ith the notation there)

===== PDF PAGE 54 =====
Proposition 6.33. Let A be an f -adic ring, let B, C be complete f -adic rings, and
let ϕ : A→B and ψ : B→C be continuous ring homomorphisms.
(1) If ϕ and ψ are (strictly) topologically of ﬁnite type, then ψ◦ϕ is (strictly) topolog-
ically of ﬁnite type.
(2) If ψ◦ϕ is topologically of ﬁnite type, then ψ is topologically of ﬁnite type.
Proof. The properties (iv) in Proposition 6.29 and (iii) in Proposi tion 6.31 imply (1).
Assertion (2) is clear.
Proposition 6.34. Let A be a Tate ring and let B be a complete f -adic ring. Then
a homomorphism ϕ : A→ B is topologically of ﬁnite type if and only if it is strictly
topologically of ﬁnite type.
Note that if these equivalent conditions are satisﬁed, then B is a Tate ring and ϕ is
adic.
Proof. LetB0 be a ring of deﬁnition of B. Then Proposition 6.25 shows that A·B0 =B.
This implies the claim by Proposition 6.29 and Proposition 6 .31.
Proposition 6.35. LetA be an f -adic ring and let B be an f -adic A-algebra topolog-
ically of ﬁnite type. If A has a noetherian ring of deﬁnition, then B has a noetherian
ring of deﬁnition.
Proof. We may assume that B = A⟨X⟩T . Let A0 be a noetherian ring of deﬁnition
of A. Then ( A0)[X] is a ring of deﬁnition of A[X]T which is ﬁnitely generated of A0
by{tiXi ; i∈ {1,...,n },ti ∈ Ti} and hence noetherian. Its completion is a ring
of deﬁnition of A⟨X⟩T and as the completion of a noetherian adic ring it is itself
noetherian.
6.7 Strongly noetherian Tate rings
Proposition and Deﬁnition 6.36. A Tate ring A is called strongly noetherian if the
following equivalent conditions are satisﬁed.
(i) ˆA⟨X1,...,X n⟩ is noetherian for all n∈ N0.
(ii) Every Tate ring topologically of ﬁnite type over A is noetherian.
Proof. The equivalence of both assertions follows immediately fro m 6.34.
Remark 6.37. (1) If A is a strongly noetherian Tate ring, then every Tate ring topo -
logically of ﬁnite type over A is strongly noetherian by Proposition 6.33.
(2) Every completely valued ﬁeld ( k,v ), where v is of height 1 (or, more generally,
microbial), is strongly noetherian ([BGR] 5.2.6 Theorem 1) . Hence every Tate ring
topologically of ﬁnite type over k is strongly noetherian as well.
(3) Every Tate ring that has a noetherian ring of deﬁnition is strongly noetherian.

===== PDF PAGE 55 =====

7.1 The space Spv(A, I)
In this subsection we denote by A a ring and by I an ideal of A such that there exists

Recall from Deﬁnition 1.16 that an element γ of a totally ordered group Γ is called
coﬁnal for a subgroup H if for all h∈H there exists n∈ N with γn <h .
55
===== PDF PAGE 56 =====
Lemma 7.1. Let v : A→ Γv∪{ 0} be a valuation on A. Let H ⊆ Γv be a convex
subgroup such that cΓv ⊊ H. Then c :={a∈A ; v(a) is coﬁnal for H} is an ideal of
A and rad(c) = c.
Proof. If a ∈ c and b ∈ A with v(b) < v(a), then b ∈ c. Hence if a,b ∈ c, then
v(a +b)≤ max{v(a),v (b)} shows that a +b∈ c. Let b∈ A, a∈ c. If v(b)≤ 1 then
v(ba)≤v(a) and hence ba∈ c. If v(b)> 1, then v(b)∈cΓv and hence v(ab) = v(a)v(b)
is coﬁnal for H because cΓv ⊊ H (Remark 1.20). The property rad( c) = c is clear.
Lemma 7.2. Let v : A→ Γv∪{ 0} be a valuation on A with v(I)∩cΓv =∅. Then
there exists a greatest convex subgroup H of Γv such that v(a) is coﬁnal for H for all
a∈I. Moreover, if v(I)̸={0}, then H⊇cΓv and H is the smallest convex subgroup
of Γv such that v(I)∩H̸=∅.
Note that v(I)∩cΓv =∅ is equivalent to v(a)<c Γv for all a∈I (if v(a)≥ 1 then
v(a)∈ cΓv and if 1 ≥ v(a)≥ γ for some γ∈ cΓv, then v(a)∈ cΓv because cΓv is by
deﬁnition convex).
Proof. If v(I) = {0}, we may choose H = Γ v. Thus we may assume that v(I)̸={0}
and hence cΓv̸= Γv.
Note that for any subgroup H of Γ v one has v(I)∩H =∅⇔ v(

I)∩H =∅.
This and Lemma 7.1 shows that we may assume that I is ﬁnitely generated. Let T be
a ﬁnite set of generators of I and let H be the convex subgroup of Γ v generated by
h := max{v(t) ; t∈T}. As remarked above v(h)< 1 and hence h is coﬁnal for H and
hence v(t) is coﬁnal for H for all t∈T . Moreover h < cΓv < h−1 as remarked before
the proof and hence cΓv ⊊ H because H is convex. Thus Lemma 7.1 shows that v(a)
is coﬁnal for H for all a∈I. If H′ is any other convex subgroup of Γ v such that v(a)
is coﬁnal for H′ for all a∈I. Then in particular h is coﬁnal for H′ which implies that
H′⊆H.
Clearly one has v(I)∩H̸=∅. Let H′ be a convex subgroup of Γ v withH′∩v(I)̸=∅.
We show that H⊆ H′. As the set of totally convex subgroups is totally ordered an d
as cΓv∩v(I) = ∅, we necessarily have cΓv ⊊ H′. Choose a∈ I with v(a)∈ H′ and
write a = ∑
t∈Tatt with at∈A. Hence
v(a)≤ max{v(at)v(t) ; t∈T}.
Let t′∈ T with v(a)≤ v(at′ )v(t′). If v(at′ )≤ 1, then v(a)≤ v(t′) < 1 which implies
v(t′)∈ H′ (as H′ is convex). If v(at′ ) > 1, then v(at′ )∈ cΓv⊆ H′ and hence H′∋
v(at′ )−1v(a)≤v(t′)< 1 which again shows that t′∈H. By deﬁnition of h and again
by the convexity of H′ we get h∈H′. Thus H⊆H′.
The lemma allows us to deﬁne for every valuation v onA the following subgroup of
Γv.
Deﬁnition 7.3. Let cΓv(I) be the group cΓv if v(I)∩cΓv̸=∅. Otherwise let cΓv(I)
be the greatest convex subgroup H of Γ v such that v(a) is coﬁnal for H for all a∈I.
Then cΓv(I) is a convex subgroup of Γ v which always contains cΓv.

Remark 7.6. The inclusion Spv(A,I )֒→Spv(A) is not spectral in general
7.2 The spectrum of continuous valuations
Deﬁnition 7.7. LetA be a topological ring, let v be a valuation on A and let Γ be its
valuation group. Then v is called continuous if it{a∈A ; v(a)<γ} is open in A for
all γ∈ Γ (i.e., the topology of A is ﬁner than the topology deﬁned by v).
We denote by Cont(A) the subspace of Spv( A) of continuous valuations on A
Remark 7.8. Let A be a topological ring.
(1) A valuation v on A is continuous, if and only if the map v : A → Γv∪{ 0} is
continuous, where Γ v∪{ 0} is endowed with the topology deﬁned in Remark 1.17.
(2) If A carries the discrete topology, then Cont( A) = Spv(A).
58
===== PDF PAGE 59 =====
(3) A valuation v on A is continuous if and only if for all γ∈ Γv the set A≤γ :={a∈
A ; v(a)≥γ} is open in A.
Indeed, if v is continuous, then the subgroup A≤γ contains the open subgroup
A<γ. Conversely, A<γ = ⋃
δ<γA≤δ.
Remark 7.9. Clearly any continuous homomorphism ϕ : A→ B of topological rings
induces a continuous morphism Cont( B)→ Cont(B) by composition with ϕ.
Theorem 7.10. Let A be an f -adic ring. Let I be an ideal of deﬁnition of a ring of
deﬁnition of A. Then
Cont(A) ={v∈ Spv(A,I·A) ; v(a)< 1 for all a∈I}.
Proof. Let v∈ Cont(A). Every element a∈I is topologically nilpotent and hence for
every γ∈ Γ there exists n∈ N such that an∈{ f∈ A ; v(f ) < γ}, i.e. v(a)n < γ.
Hence v(a) is coﬁnal for Γ w. This shows v(a) < 1 and that cΓv(I· A) = Γ v by
Lemma 7.4 (iii). Hence v∈{ v∈ Spv(A,I·A) ; v(a)< 1 for all a∈I}.
Conversely, let v ∈ Spv(A,I · A) with v(a) < 1 for all a ∈ I. We claim that
v(a) is coﬁnal for Γ v for all a∈ I. Indeed, if cΓv̸= Γ v, then the claim follows from
Lemma 7.4. Thus we may assume that cΓv = Γ v. Let a∈ I and γ∈ Γv be given.
By our assumptions there exists t∈ A with v(t)̸= 0 and v(t)−1≤ γ. Choose n∈ N
such that tan∈ I (this exists because {t} is bounded). Then v(tan) < 1 and hence
v(a)n <γ . This proves the claim.
Let γ ∈ Γ be given. Let T be a ﬁnite system of generators of I and set δ :=
max{v(t) ; t∈ T}. By the claim above there exists n∈ N such that δn < γ. Then
v(a)<γ for all a∈Tn·I =In+1. Hence v is continuous.
Remark 7.11. LetA be an f -adic ring, I an ideal of deﬁnition of a ring of deﬁnition
of A.
(1) The proof of Theorem 7.10 shows that a valuation v onA is continuous if and only
if v(a) is coﬁnal for Γ v for all a∈I.
(2) Let v be a continuous valuation on A and let H ⊊ Γv be a proper convex subgroup.
For a∈ I the image of v(a) in Γ v/H is coﬁnal for Γ v/H (Corollary 1.21). This
shows that the vertical generalization v/H of v is still continuous.
Corollary 7.12. LetA be an f -adic ring. Then Cont(A) is a spectral space.
Proof. LetI be the ideal of A generated by an ideal of deﬁnition of a ring of deﬁnition of
A. Then Cont(A) is the complement of the open subset ⋃
f∈I Spv(A,I )( 1
f ) of Spv(A,I ).
Hence Cont(A) is closed in Spv( A,I ) and hence a spectral space because Spv( A,I ) is
a spectral space by Lemma 7.5.
Example 7.13. Let K be a ﬁeld, let v be a valuation on K and endow K with the
topology induced by v. Then Cont K ={w∈ SpvK ; w dependent on v}.

7.3 Aﬃnoid rings
For a subring B of a ring A we denote by Bint the integral closure of B in A.
Deﬁnition 7.14. (1) Let A be an f -adic ring and let Ao be the subring of power-
bounded elements. A subring B ofA is called ring of integral elements ifB is open
and integrally closed in A and if B⊆Ao.
(2) An aﬃnoid ring is a pair ( A,A +), where A is an f -adic ring and where A+ is a ring
of integral elements. Often we will simply write A instead of (A,A +).
(3) An aﬃnoid ring ( A,A +) is called complete (resp. adic, resp. Tate, resp. ...) if A
has this property.
(4) A morphism of aﬃnoid rings ( A,A +)→ (B,B +) is a ring homomorphism ϕ : A→
B such that ϕ(A+)⊆ B+. It is called continuous (resp. adic) if ϕ : A→ B is
continuous (resp. adic).
Remark 7.15. Let A be an f -adic ring.
(1) Then Ao is a ring of integral elements (Proposition 5.30). It is clea rly the largest
ring of integral elements.
(2) If ˜A is any subring of A which is integrally closed in A, then Aoo⊆ ˜A if and only
if ˜A is open in A. Indeed, if ˜A is open in A, then for all a∈ Aoo there exists an
n∈ N such that an∈ ˜A. But then a∈ ˜A because ˜A is integrally closed in A. If ˜A
containsAoo, then for every ring of deﬁnition A0 ofA and any ideal of deﬁnition I
of A0 one has I⊆Aoo⊆ ˜A. Hence ˜A is open.
Thus if A′ is the integral closure of Z· 1 +Aoo inA, then A′ is the smallest ring
of integral elements of A.
(3) If ˜A is an subring of A with Aoo⊆ ˜A⊆Ao, then its integral closure A+ in A is a
ring of integral elements by (2).
Example 7.16. LetA be an adic ring which has a ﬁnitely generated ideal of deﬁniti on.
Then (A,A ) is an aﬃnoid ring.
Example 7.17. Let K be a ﬁeld endowed with the topology given by a microbial
valuation v on K and let v1 be the unique equivalence class of a height 1 valuation
dependent on v. Then ( K,A (v)) is a Tate aﬃnoid ring and Ko =A(v1).
Let A be an f -adic ring. For every subset X⊆A we set
SX :={v∈ Cont(A) ; v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈X}.
As S{a} = Spv(A,I )( 1,a
1 )∩ Cont(A) (I an ideal of deﬁnition of a ring of deﬁnition of
A) is constructible for all a∈A, SX is pro-constructible for all subsets X⊆A.
Proposition 7.18. We set
SA :={SX ; X⊆A}
and denote by RA the set of subrings of A which are open and integrally closed in
A.

(1) The maps
σ :RA→S A, A ′↦→{v∈ ContA ; v(f )≤ 1 for all f∈A′},
τ :SA→R A, S ↦→{f∈A ; v(f )≤ 1 for all v∈S},
are mutually inverse bijections.
(2) LetA+ be an element of RA with A+⊆Ao, i.e., A+ is a ring of integral elements.
Then every point of Cont(A) is a vertical specialization of a point in σ(A+) . In
particular,σ(A+) is dense in ContA.
(3) If A is a Tate ring and has a noetherian ring of deﬁnition, then als o the converse
of (2) does hold: if A′∈R A such that σ(A′) is dense in ContA then A′⊆Ao.
Proof. [Hu2] Lemma 3.3
Proposition 7.19. Let A = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring and let (Ti)i∈I be a ﬁnite

If A is complete and Ti is ﬁnite for all i∈ I, then A⟨X⟩T is complete by Proposi-

Proof. As A+ is open in A, then A+

Lemma 7.20. LetA be a f -adic ring and let (Ti)i∈I be a family of subsets of A such
that Ti·A is open in A for all i. Then (Ao)⟨X⟩⊆ (A⟨X⟩T )o.
It is not diﬃcult to see that one has equality in Lemma 7.20 if I is ﬁnite and Ti ={1}
for all i.
Proof. Let a∈ (Ao)⟨X⟩ and write a =b +c, where b∈ (Ao)[X] and c∈J⟨X⟩, where J
is an ideal of deﬁnition of A. Then b and c are both power-bounded. Therefore a is
power-bounded.
Remark 7.21. With the notation of Proposition 7.19 the canonical homomor phism

homomorphism to a complete aﬃnoid ring B and let bi∈B fori∈I such that ϕ(t)bi∈
B+ for all i∈ I and t∈ Ti. Then there exists a unique continuous homomorphism

Remark and Deﬁnition 7.22. Let A = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring and let I be an
ideal of A. Then A/I := (A/I, (A+/(A+∩I))int) is an aﬃnoid ring, if A/I is endowed
with the quotient topology. If A0⊆A is a ring of deﬁnition, its image in A/I is a ring
of deﬁnition in A/I. The canonical morphism A→A/I is an adic morphism of aﬃnoid

7.4 The adic spectrum as topological space
Deﬁnition 7.23. Let A = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring. The adic spectrum of A is the
subspace
SpaA ={v∈ Cont(A) ; v(f )≤ 1 for all f∈A+}
of Cont(A).
IfA is an f -adic ring and A′ is the integral closure of Z· 1 +Aoo inA, then one has
Spa(A,A′) = ContA.
Remark 7.24. Let A be a ring, let ˜A⊆A be any subring, and let A+ be its integral
closure in A. Then for any v∈ SpvA one has
v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈A+⇔v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈ ˜A
Indeed if a∈ A+, then an +bn−1an−1 +··· +b0 = 0 with bi∈ ˜A. Assume v(a) > 1
but v(bi)≤ 1 for all i. Then for all i<n one has v(an) = v(a)n >v (a)i≥v(biai) and
hence v(an)>v (bn−1an−1 +··· +b0); contradiction.
Thus if A isf -adic and ˜A is a subring of A such that its integral closure A+ satisﬁes
Aoo⊆A+⊆Ao (i.e., A+ is a ring of integral elements), then
Spa(A,A +) ={v∈ Cont(A) ; v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈ ˜A}.
Remark 7.25. Let (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring. Then Proposition 7.18 shows that for
every pointv of Cont(A) there exists a vertical generization x which is in Spa(A,A +). In
fact, if suppv is open we can simply take for x the trivial valuation with suppx = suppv.
If supp v is not open, then it follows from Remark 7.11 ((2)) and Propos ition 7.41
below that there exists a vertical generization x ofv of height 1 (necessarily unique by
Remark 4.12) such that x∈ Spa(A,A +).
Example 7.26. Let A be a discrete topological ring and let A+ any subring of A
which is integrally closed in A (e.g., A+ = A). Then ( A,A +) is an aﬃnoid ring and
Spa(A,A +) ={v∈ SpvA ; v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈A+}.
Remark 7.27. Let A be an aﬃnoid ring. For x∈ SpaA and f ∈ A we sometimes
write|f (x)| instead of x(f ).
Remark and Deﬁnition 7.28. Any continuous morphismϕ : A→B between aﬃnoid
rings deﬁnes via composition a continuous map Spa( ϕ) : Spa B → SpaA. Thus we
obtain a contravariant functor from the category of aﬃnoid r ings to the category of
topological spaces.

Example 7.37. Let K be a ﬁeld, endowed with the topology induced by a microbial

Proposition 7.38. LetA = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring and let a⊂A be an ideal. Let

the closed subset of points x∈ SpaA with supp(x)⊇ a.
Not all closed subspaces of Spa A are of this form.
7.5 Analytic Points
Deﬁnition 7.39. LetA be a topological ring. A point x∈ ContA is called analytic if
suppx is not open in A.
If A = (A,A +) is an aﬃnoid ring, then the subset of analytic points in Spa A is
denoted by (Spa A)a, its complement in Spa A is denoted by (Spa A)na.
Any valuation with open support is automatically continuou s.
Remark 7.40. Let A = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring.
(1) For x∈ Cont(A) the following assertions are equivalent.
(i) x has non-open support.
(ii) There exists a∈ (A)oo such that x(a)̸= 0.
(2) (Spa A)a is an open quasi-compact subset of Spa A.
(3) If A is Tate, then Cont A = (ContA)a and SpaA = (SpaA)a.
(4) In (Cont A)a (and hence in (Spa A)a) there are no proper horizontal specializations.
Thus all specializations are vertical.
(5) Every valuation x in (ContA)a has height≥ 1 and is microbial.
(6) Let x∈ (ContA)a. Then x has height = 1 if and only if x is a maximal point of
(SpaA)a (i.e., the closure of {x} in (SpaA)a is an irreducible component).
Proof. Assertion (1) follows from Lemma 6.6, which in particular sh ows
{ p∈ SpecA ; p is open} =V ((A)oo).
Let T⊆ (A)oo be a ﬁnite subset such that T·A is open in A (e.g., if T is a system of
generators of an ideal of deﬁnition of a ring of deﬁnition of A). Then
(SpaA)a =
⋃
t∈T
R(T
t ).
65
===== PDF PAGE 66 =====
This shows (2). Assertion (3) follows from (1).
Letv be a continuous valuation on A with value group Γ, and let v|H be a horizontal
specialization, where H is a proper convex subgroup of Γ (containing cΓv). Then
supp(v|H) is the union of {a∈ A ; v(a) < γ}, where γ runs through all elements of
Γ\H. Thus v|H is non-analytic. This shows (4).
A continuous valuation x of height 0 is a trivial valuation whose support is {a∈
A ; x(a)< 1} and hence it is open. Assume that x is analytic, i.e., A/(suppx) endowed
with the quotient topology is not discrete. Then there exist s by ((1)) a topologically
nilpotent element ̸= 0 in K(x). Thus the valuation x on K(x) is microbial. This
shows (5).
Let us prove (6). By (4) it remains to show that an analytic con tinuous valuation
x has height 1 if there exists no proper vertical generization of x. But the vertical
generizations are those within Spv K(x) (Remark 4.12).
Proposition 7.41. LetA be an f -adic ring and let x∈ Cont(A)a be of height 1. Then
x(a)≤ 1 for all a∈ Ao. In particular x∈ Spa(A,A +)a for every ring of integral
elements A+.
Proof. Let a∈Ao and assume that x(a)> 1. Choose b∈Aoo with x(b)̸= 0 (possible
by Remark 7.40 ((1))). As Γ x has height 1, it is archimedean (Proposition 1.14). Hence
there exists n∈ N with x(an) > x(b)−1, i.e., x(anb) > 1. But as a is power-bounded,
anb∈Aoo and thus the continuity of x implies that x(anb)< 1. Contradiction.
Remark 7.42. Let A = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring and let x∈ SpaA.
(1) Let H ⊊ Γx be a proper convex subgroup. Then the vertical generization x/H is a
point of Spa A.
Indeed, x/H is continuous by Remark 7.11 ((2)), and if x(a)≤ 1 for all a∈A+
then clearly x/H (a)≤ 1 for all a∈A+.
(2) If x is analytic, it is microbial (Remark 7.40 ((5))) and hence th ere exists by (1) a
vertical generization of height 1.
(3) If x is non-analytic, then the vertical generization x/Γ x is the trivial valuation with
support supp(x). As supp( x) is an open prime ideal, x/Γ x is continuous and hence
a point of Spa A.
Example 7.43. Let A be an aﬃnoid ring and let x ∈ SpaA. Endow K(x) =
FracA/(suppx) with the topology induced by x. As x is continuous, the canonical
ring homomorphism γ : A→K(x) is continuous.
Assume that x is analytic. By Remark 7.40 ((5)) the valuation x onK(x) is micro-
bial. Thus ( K(x),A (x)) is an aﬃnoid ring and the condition |f (x)|≤ 1 for all f∈A+
then implies, that γ is a morphism of aﬃnoid rings.
Lemma 7.44. LetA be an f -adic ring and let B be an open subring of A. Denote by
f : Spec A→ SpecB andg : Spv A→ SpvB be the morphisms induced by the inclusion
֒→A.
(1) Set T :={ q∈ SpecB ; q open}. Then f−1(T ) ={ p∈ SpecA ; p open} and the
restrictionf : (Spec A)\f−1(T )→ (SpecB)\T is a homeomorphism.
66
===== PDF PAGE 67 =====
(2) Cont(A) = g−1(Cont(B)).
(3) g yields by restriction a homeomorphism Cont(A)a
∼→ Cont(B)a and one has for
every v∈ Cont(A)a that Γv = Γg(v).
Proof. (1). AsB is open in A, a prime ideal p ofA is open if and only if p∩B is open
in B. This shows f−1(T ) ={ p∈ SpecA ; p open}.
Let q⊆B be a non-open prime ideal. Then there exists s∈Boo with s /∈ q. As B
is open, there exists for all a∈A an integer n≥ 1 such that sna∈B. This shows that
the injective homomorphism Bs→As is also surjective. This shows the claim because
(SpecB)\T = ⋃
s∈Boo SpecBs and f−1(SpecBs) = Spec As.
(2). Clearly one has Cont( A)⊆ g−1(Cont(B)). Let v be a valuation on A such
that w := v|B is continuous. If supp( v) is open in A, then v is continuous. Assume
that p := supp(v) is not open in A and set q :=B∩ p. Then (1) shows that Bq→Ap is
an isomorphism and in particular Γ v = Γw. Hence the subgroup {a∈A ; v(a) < γ}
contains the open subgroup {b∈ B ; w(b) < γ} for all γ∈ Γv = Γ w. Therefore v is
continuous.
(3). This follows from (1) and the proof of (2).
Lemma 7.45. Let A be a complete aﬃnoid ring. Let p be a non-open prime ideal of
A. Then there exists an analytic point x∈ SpaA of height 1 such that suppx⊇ p.
If A has a noetherian ring of deﬁnition, we may assume in addition that x is a
discrete valuation and that suppx = p.
Proof. Let A0 be a ring of deﬁnition of A and let I⊆A0 be a ﬁnitely generated ideal
of deﬁnition. Set p0 := p∩A0. As p0 is not open, one has I̸⊆p0.
Construction of x in the general case. Let m be a maximal ideal of A0 containing
p0. As A0 is complete for the I-adic topology, one has I ⊆ m (Proposition 5.38).
In particular p0 ⊊ m. Let u0 be a valuation of Frac A0/p0 such that its valuation
ring dominates ( A0/p0)m/p0. Let u be the corresponding valuation of A0 such that
suppu = p0. Then u(a)< 1 for all a∈ m.
Let r : Spv(B0)→ Spv(B0,I ) be the retraction from (7.1.2). As u(a) < 1 for all
a∈I one also has r(u)< 1 for all a∈I by deﬁnition of r(u). Hence r(u)∈ Cont(B0)
by Theorem 7.10. Moreover by Lemma 7.5 ((3)) one has I̸⊆supp(r(u)). Hence r(u) is
not analytic. By Lemma 7.44 ((3)) there exists a unique conti nuous analytic valuation
v on A such that v|A0 =r(u). Then supp( v)∩A0 = suppr(u)⊇ suppu = p0 because
r(u) is a (horizontal) specialization of u.
By Remark 7.40 ((5)) v is microbial and thus there exists a vertical generalizatio nx
of v of height 1 within Spv A (Remark 4.12). Then x is continuous Remark 7.11 ((2)).
As x is of height 1, Proposition 7.41 shows that x∈ SpaA.
Construction of x if A0 is noetherian. Let A0 be noetherian. Let J be the image
ofI inA0/p0. Let π : Z→X := Spec(A0/p0) be the blow-up of X inY :=V (J). As J
contains a regular element, π is projective and birational, and π−1(Y ) is a non-empty
divisor of Z. As A0 is noetherian, Z is noetherian. Thus if z is a maximal point of
π−1(Y ), then OZ,z is a local noetherian ring of dimension 1. By the theorem of Kr ull-
Akizuki its normalization is a discrete valuation ring R. We have Frac R = FracA0/p0
because π is birational. Let w be the valuation on A0 with suppw = p0 corresponding
67
===== PDF PAGE 68 =====
to R. Then w(a) < 1 for all a∈ I. As Γ w is of height 1 (in fact isomorphic to the
totally ordered group Z) this implies that w(a) is coﬁnal for Γ w for all a∈ I. Hence
w is continuous (Remark 7.11). As I̸⊆p0, w is analytic. Therefore Lemma 7.44 ((3))
shows that there exists a unique continuous valuation x∈ Cont(A) with x|A0 = w.
Moreover Γw = Γx and hence x is a discrete valuation. Again Proposition 7.41 shows
that x∈ SpaA.
Lemma 7.46. Let ϕ : A→ B be a continuous homomorphism between aﬃnoid rings

same radical. Therefore there exists a prime ideal p of B0 such that ϕ(I)⊆ p and
J ̸⊆p (i.e., p is non-open). By Lemma 7.45 there exists a point x∈ X a such that
(suppx)∩B0⊇ p. But supp f (x) = ϕ−1(suppx)⊇I and hence f (x) /∈Y a.
(3). Let s∈ A and T ⊆ A a ﬁnite subset such that T·A is open in A. If ϕ is
adic, then ϕ(T )·B is open in B. Hence f−1(R(T
s )) is the rational subset R(ϕ(T )

Proposition 7.48. LetA be an aﬃnoid ring. The canonical map Spa ˆA→ SpaA is a

Proof. [Hu2] Prop. 3.9
Proposition 7.49. LetA = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring.
(1) The following assertions are equivalent.
(i) Spa A =∅.
(ii) Cont(A) =∅.
(iii) A/{0} = 0.
(2) The following assertions are equivalent.
(i) (Spa A)a =∅.
(ii) (Cont A)a =∅.
(iii) The Hausdorﬀ ring A/{0} attached to A has the discrete topology.
The equivalent conditions in (1) (resp. in (2)) imply that ˆA = 0 (resp. that ˆA is
discrete).
Proof. First note that in (1) and (2) the implications “(iii) ⇒ (ii)⇒ (i)” are clear. We
set X := Spa A. Let B be a ring of deﬁnition of A and let I be a ﬁnitely generated
ideal of deﬁnition of B.
(2). Assume that X a =∅.
We ﬁrst claim that if p⊆ q are prime ideals of B and I⊆ q, then I⊆ p. The proof
is similar as the proof of the general case of Lemma 7.45. Assu me that I̸⊆p. Let u
be a valuation of B such that supp u = p and such that the valuation ring B(u) of u
dominates the local ring ( B/p)q/p. Let r : Spv B→ Spv(B,I ) be the retraction (7.1.2).
Then r(u) is a continuous valuation with I ⊊ supp(r(u)). By Lemma 7.44 there exists
a continuous valuation v on A with r(u) = v|B. The vertical generization w of v of
height 1 is then an element of X a. Contradiction.
We now prove (2) using the claim. Set S := 1 + I and let ϕ : B→ C := S−1B
the localization. As in Spec C every prime ideal specializes to a prime ideal containing
ϕ(I), the claim implies that ϕ(I) is contained in every prime ideal of C. Hence there
exists n∈ N with ϕ(In) = 0 because I is ﬁnitely generated. This means that there
exists i∈I with (1 + i)In = 0 in B. But then Ik =In for all k≥n. This shows that
A/
{0} is discrete.
(1). Assume that X =∅. Then by (2) the ideal {0} is open in A. Then every
trivial valuation whose support contains {0} is an element of X. This shows that A
has no prime ideal containing {0}, i.e. A ={0}.
Remark 7.50. Let A be a complete aﬃnoid ring such that (Spa A)a =∅. Then A is
discrete by Proposition 7.49. This can also be seen as follow s. Let ( B,I ) be a pair of
deﬁnition of A. By Lemma 7.45 all prime ideals of B are open. Thus I is contained in
all prime ideals of B and hence in the nil radical of B. As I is ﬁnitely generated, this
showsIn = 0.
Proposition 7.51. Let A be a complete aﬃnoid ring and let m⊂ A be a maximal
ideal. Then m is closed and there exists v∈ SpaA with suppv = m.
69
===== PDF PAGE 70 =====
Proof. The set of (A)oo of topologically nilpotent elements of an f -adic ring is open (as
the union of all deﬁnition ideals of all deﬁnition rings). He nce 1 +Aoo is open in A. As
A is complete, 1 + Aoo is a subgroup of the group of units of A. This shows that A×
is open in A. If m was not closed, then its closure (being an ideal) would be A, which
contradicts that A\A× is closed. Hence m is closed.
Thus A/m is a Hausdorﬀ and {v ∈ SpaA ; supp( v) = m} = Spa A/m̸=∅ by
Proposition 7.49.

Proof. Assertion (1) is simply a reformulation of Proposition 7.18 (1). Assertion (2) is

7.7 Examples of adic spectra
Deﬁnition 7.56. A non-archimedean ﬁeld is a topological ﬁeld k whose topology is
induced by a valuation of height 1.
An aﬃnoid k-algebra topologically of ﬁnite type (tft for short) is an aﬃnoid ring
A = (A,A +) such that A is a k-algebra topologically of ﬁnite type and such that
A+ =Ao.
Every non-archimedean ﬁeld has then a continuous valuation |·| : k→ R≥0 which is
unique up to passing from|·| to|·| t for somet∈ R>0. Moreover, ko ={c∈k ;|c|≤ 1}
and koo ={c∈k ; |c|< 1}.
Example 7.57. Letk be a non-archimedean ﬁeld and ﬁx the norm |·| : k→ R≥0. Let
|k×| be its value group. For x∈k andr∈ R≥0 we set D(x,r ) :={y∈k ;|y−x|≤ r}.
Such subsets are called discs. Note that for any x′∈D(x,r ) one has D(x′,r ) = D(x,r ).
We also setDo(x,r ) :={y∈k ;|x−y|<r}. Then koo =D0(0, 1) is the maximal ideal
ofko and we denote by κ =ko/koo the residue ﬁeld. We assume that k is algebraically
closed and complete. Then κ is also algebraically closed.
LetA =k⟨t⟩ be the ring of convergent power series in one variable and A+ =Ao =
ko⟨t⟩. In X = Spa(A,Ao) there are 5 diﬀerent kind of points. We visualize X as a
tree.
(1) The classical points (end points): Let x∈ ko, i.e., x∈ D(0, 1). Then for any
f∈k⟨t⟩ we can evaluate f atx to get a map k⟨t⟩→ k,f↦→f (x). Composing with
the norm on k, one gets a valuation f↦→|f (x)| on k⟨t⟩, which is continuous and
≤ 1 for all f∈R+. These classical points correspond to the maximal ideals of A:
If m⊂A is a maximal ideal, then A/m =k ([BGR] 6.1.2 Corollary 3; because k is
algebraically closed) and m is of the form ( t−x) for a unique x∈D(0, 1).
These are the end points of the branches.
(2),(3) Points on the limbs: Let 0 ≤r≤ 1 be some real number and x∈ko. Then

Example 7.58. Let k be a complete non-archimedean ﬁeld and let |·| : k→ R≥0 be
a height 1 valuation. Let A be a k-algebra topologically of ﬁnite type over k. Every
maximal ideal element m in
Max(A) :={ m⊂A ; m maximal ideal}
deﬁnes a point of X := Spa(A,Ao) (again called “classical point”): For x∈ Max(A)
the quotient A/x is a ﬁnite extension of k by Proposition 5.61. Thus there exists a
unique extension of |·| toA/x. Its composition with A→A/m is denoted by|·| x and
it is a point of X. Via x↦→|·|x we consider Max(A) as a subset of X. We also set
LA :={v∈ SpvA ; v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈Ao and v(a)< 1 for all a∈Aoo}.
Then
Max(A)⊆ Spa(A,Ao)⊆LA⊆ SpvA.

One can show that LA is the closure of Max A in the constructible topology of Spv( A).
Instead of Ao we can also consider the following ring of integral elements A+ which
is deﬁned to be the integral closure of ko +Aoo. Then A+ is the smallest ring of integral
elements of A which contains ko. Then Xc := Spa(A,A +) contains X as an open dense
subset.
Example 7.59 (cf. [Hu1] (3.7.1)) . LetA be an adic ring with a ﬁnitely generated ideal
of deﬁnition I. We consider
X = Spa(A,A ) ={x∈ Cont(A) ; x(f )≤ 1 for all f∈A}.
Let Y = Xtriv be the subspace of trivial valuations. Such a trivial valuat ion is
continuous if and only if its support is an open prime ideal. T hus we can identify
Y = Spf A :={ p∈ SpecA ; p open in A}. It follows from Remark 4.6 that Y carries
also the subspace topology of Spec A.
The subspace Y is pro-constructible, in particular Y is spectral and the inclusion
֒→X is spectral. There is a spectral retraction r : X→Y , given by x↦→x|cΓ x.
Once we have deﬁned the structure sheaf OX on X, we will see that attaching to
a complete noetherian adic ring A the adic space Spa( A,A ) yields an embedding t
of the category of noetherian formal aﬃne schemes into the ca tegory of adic spaces.
The inverse functor (on the essential image of t) is given by attaching to ( X, OX ) the
topologically ringed space ( Xtriv,r∗OX).

Proposition 8.6. For every x∈ X = Spa A, the stalk OX,x is a local ring and the
maximal ideal of OX,x is the support of vx.
Proof. LetU⊆ SpaA be an open subset with x∈U and letf∈ OX(U ) withvx(f )̸= 0.

We denote by k(x) the residue ﬁeld of the local ring OX,x. The valuation vx induces
a valuation on k(x) which is again denoted by vx. Its valuation ring is denoted by
k(x)+.
We denote by V pre the category of tuples X = (X, OX, (vx)x∈X), where
(a) X is a topological space,
(b) OX is a presheaf of complete topological rings on X such that the stalk OX,x of
OX (considered as a presheaf of rings) is a local ring,
(c) vx is an equivalence class of valuations on the stalk OX,x such that supp(vx) is the
maximal ideal of OX,x.
The morphisms f : X→Y are pairs (f,f ♭), where f is a continuous map of topological
spaces X→Y and f♭ : OY→f∗OX is a morphisms of pre-sheaves of topological rings
(i.e., for all V ⊆ Y open, ϕV : OY (V )→ OX(f−1(V )) is a continuous ring homomor-
phism) such that for all x∈X the induced ring homomorphism f♭
x : OY,f (x)→ OX,x is

compatible with the valuation vx andvf (x), i.e., vf (x) =vx◦f♭

We have seen above that for an aﬃnoid ring A its adic spectrum Spa A is an object
in V pre.

Remark and Deﬁnition 8.10. An aﬃnoid pre-adic space is an object of V pre which
is isomorphic to Spa A for an aﬃnoid ring A.
LetX be an object in V pre such that there exists an open covering ( Ui)i of X such
that (U, OX|U, (vx)x∈U ) is an aﬃnoid pre-adic space. An open subset U of X is called
open aﬃnoid subspace if (U, OX|U, (vx)x∈U ) is an aﬃnoid pre-adic space. The open
aﬃnoid subspaces form a basis of the topology of the underlyi ng topological space of
X.
We callX a pre-adic space if in addition the sheaf of topological rings OX is adapted
to the basis of open aﬃnoid subspaces. The full subcategory o f V pre of pre-adic spaces
is called the category of pre-adic spaces and it is denoted by (PreAd).
For every aﬃnoid ring A, SpaA is a pre-adic space by deﬁnition.
Remark 8.11. Let X be a pre-adic space.
(1) For every open subset U⊆X the object (U, OX|U, (vx)x∈U ) in V is again a pre-adic
space.
(2) Let U and V be open aﬃnoid adic subspaces of X. Then for all x∈U∩V there
exists an open neighborhood W⊆U∩V of x such that W is a rational subset of
U and of V .

Lemma 8.14. Let X and Y be pre-adic spaces. Let (f,f ♭) : (X, OX )→ (Y, OY ) be
a pair such that f : X → Y is continuous and such that f♭ : OY → f∗OX is a local

Y )⊆f∗O +
X.
(b) The induced morphism O +

Proof. It is clear that the condition is necessary. Assume that cond ition (a) and (b) are

and (8.1.6) show that Af (x)⊆Ax, and Condition (b) shows that Ax dominates Af (x).
Hence Ax =Af (x).

8.2 Adic spaces
Remark 8.20. Let X be a topological space, and let OX be a presheaf of topological
rings (i.e. OX (U ) is a topological ring for U⊆ X open and the restriction maps are
continuous). Then OX is a sheaf of topological rings (i.e., for every topological ringT ,
U↦→Hom(T, OX(U )) is a sheaf of sets on X) if and only if OX is a sheaf of rings and
for every open subset U of X and for every open covering ( Ui)i∈I of U the canonical
map OX (U )→ ∏
i∈I OX(Ui) is a topological embedding (where the product is endowed
with the product topology).
Let V be the full subcategory of V pre consisting of those objects ( X, OX, (vx)x∈X )
in V pre such that OX is a sheaf of topological rings.
LetA be an aﬃnoid ring, set X := SpaA, and for x∈X letvx be the valuation de-
ﬁned on OX,x in Deﬁnition 8.5. If OX is a sheaf of topological rings, then ( X, OX, (vx)x)
is an object of V which we again denote by Spa A.
Deﬁnition 8.21. An aﬃnoid adic space is an object of V which is isomorphic to
X := SpaA for some aﬃnoid ring A such that OX is a sheaf of topological rings.
Deﬁnition 8.22. An adic space is an object X of V such that there exists an open
covering (Ui)i∈I ofX such that (Ui, O|Ui, (vx)x∈Ui) is an aﬃnoid adic space for all i∈I.
The full subcategory of V of adic spaces is denoted by (Adic).
Remark 8.23. Let X be an adic space.
(1) The open aﬃnoid adic subspaces form a basis of the topolog y of X.
(2) For every open subset U⊆X the object ( U, OX|U, (vx)x∈U ) in V is again an adic
space.
(3) Let U and V be open aﬃnoid adic subspaces of X. Then for all x∈U∩V there
exists an open neighborhood W⊆U∩V of x such that W is a rational subset of
U and of V .
Remark 8.24. The morphisms of adic spaces form a sheaf. More precisely, if X and
Y are adic spaces, then the presheaf on X of sets
U↦→Hom(Adic)(U,Y ),
with the obvious restriction maps, is a sheaf.
For two pairs ( A,A′), (B,B′) consisting of a topological ring A (resp. B) and a
subringA′ ofA (resp.B′ ofB) we denote by Hom((A,A′), (B,B′)) the set of continuous
ring homomorphisms ϕ : A→B such that ϕ(A′)⊆B′.
Proposition 8.25. LetX be an adic space, let A be an aﬃnoid ring and set Y = SpaA.

Deﬁnition 8.26. We call an f -adic ring A sheafy, if for every ring A+ of integral
elements of ˆA the presheaf OSpa( ˆA,A+) is a sheaf of topological rings.

Theorem 8.28. LetA = (A,A +) be an aﬃnoid ring and X = SpaA. Assume that A
satisﬁes one of the following conditions.
(a) The completion ˆA has a noetherian ring of deﬁnition.
(b) A is a strongly noetherian Tate ring.
(c) ˆA has the discrete topology.
Then OX is a sheaf of complete topological rings. Moreover, one has Hq(U, OX ) = 0
for all q≥ 1 and all rational subsets U of X.
We will give the proof only in the cases (b) and (c). In case (c) the proof is esay:
Proof in the discrete case. We may and do assume that A is complete. Assume that
A carries the discrete topology. Then A+ can be any subring of A which is integrally
closed in A, and Spa A ={v∈ SpvA ; v(f )≤ 1 for all f∈A+}.
If U = R(T
s ) is a rational subset of Spa A, then OX (U ) = As, endowed with the
discrete topology, and the OX-acyclicity of rational covers follows from the analogue
statement for the structure sheaf of an aﬃne scheme and open c overings by principally
open subsets D(s) = Spec As. In fact, in this case the structure sheaf on Spa A is simply
the pullback of the structure sheaf on Spec A under the continuous and surjective map
SpaA→ SpecA, x↦→supp(x).
For the proof if A is a strongly noetherian Tate algebra we need some preparati ons.
We will use some general results on ˇCech cohomology, recalled in Appendix A.

8.3 Analytic Points of adic spaces
Proposition and Deﬁnition 8.36. LetX be an adic space. A point x∈X is called
analytic if the following equivalent conditions are satisﬁed.
(i) There exists an open neighborhood U of x such that OX(U ) contains a topologically
nilpotent unit.
(ii) For every open aﬃnoid neighborhood U = SpaA of x, the point suppx⊂A is not
open in A (i.e., x∈ SpaA is analytic in the sense Deﬁnition 7.39.)
We set X a :={x∈X ; x is analytic} and X na :=X\X a.
Proof. We may assume that X = SpaA, A complete aﬃnoid ring.
“(ii)⇒ (i)”. Letx∈ SpaA such that suppx is not open in A. By Remark 7.40 ((1))
there exists a topologically nilpotent element s of A with x(s)̸= 0. Then U :={y∈
SpaA ; y(s)̸= 0} is an open neighborhood of x in Spa A. As the restriction A =
OX(X)→ OX (U ) is a continuous ring homomorphism, the image of s in OX (U ) is
again a topologically nilpotent unit.
“(i)⇒ (ii)”. Let x∈ SpaA such that supp x is open A, and let U be an open
neighborhood of x. We have to show that OX (U ) has no topologically nilpotent unit.
LetV be a rational subset of Spa A withx∈V⊆U and set p :={f∈ OX(V ) ; vx(f ) =
0}. Then p is a prime ideal of OX (V ) with p∩A = suppx. As supp x contains an ideal
of deﬁnition of (a ring of deﬁnition of) A, p contains an ideal of deﬁnition of OX(V ) by
deﬁnition of OX(V ). Thus p is an open prime ideal of OX (V ) and contains therefore all
topologically nilpotent elements of OX (V ). As p contains no units, OX (V ) contains no
topologically nilpotent units. Hence OX(U ) does not contain a topologically nilpotent
unit.
Remark 8.37. Let X be an adic space.
85
===== PDF PAGE 86 =====
(1) Remark 7.40 ((2)) implies that X a is an open constructible subset of X.
(2) For every open subspace U of X one has U a =X a∩U and U na =X na∩U .
8.4 Adic morphisms of adic spaces
Deﬁnition 8.38. A morphism f : X → Y of adic spaces is called adic if for every
x∈ X there exist open aﬃnoid neighborhoods U of x in X and V of f (x) in Y with
f (U )⊆V such that the ring homomorphism of f -adic rings OY (V )→ OX (U ) induced
byf is adic.
Proposition 8.39. Letf : X→Y be a morphism of adic spaces.
(1) f is a adic if and only if f (X a)⊆Y a.
(2) f (X na)⊆Y na.
Proof. It follows from Remark 8.37 ((2)) that we may assume that X andY are aﬃnoid.
But in this case we have already shown the all results in Lemma 7.46.
Corollary 8.40. Let f : X → Y be an adic morphism of adic spaces. Then for all
open aﬃnoid subspaces U ⊆ X and V ⊆ Y with f (U )⊆ V the ring homomorphism
OY (V )→ OX (U ) induced by f is adic.
Proof. It follows from Proposition 8.39 ((1)) that f (U a)⊆V a. Thus Lemma 7.46 ((2))
implies the claim.
Let X be an adic space, x ∈ X. Recall that we denote by k(x) the residue
ﬁeld of OX,x. If x is non-analytic, we endow k(x) with the discrete topology. If
x is analytic, then we endow k(x) with the topology induced by vx. Then k(x) is
an f -adic ﬁeld (Example 6.5 ((3))). In fact, this is the unique st ructure of an f -
adic ﬁeld on k(x) such that for every open aﬃnoid neighborhood U of x the canon-
ical homomorphism OX (U ) → k(x) is adic ([Hu1] 3.8.10). We also have deﬁned
k(x)+ :={a∈k(x) ; vx(a)≤ 1} and we set
κ(x) := (k(x),k (x)+)
which is an aﬃnoid ﬁeld. Moreover, the underlsing topologic al space of Spa κ(x) is
totally ordered by specialization. The closed point is vx. If x is analytic, the generic
point is the unique height 1 valuation on k(x) that is dependent on vx. If x is non-
analytic, the generic point of Spa κ(x) is the trivial valuation on k(x).
The canonical morphism ix : Spa κ(x)→ X of adic spaces is adic. It maps the
closed point of Spa κ(x) to x∈X and it yields a homeomorphism of Spa κ(x) onto the
subspace consisting of the vertical generizations of x.

Deﬁnition 8.42. A homomorphisms π : (C,C +)→ (B,B +) between aﬃnoid rings is
called a quotient mapping if π : C→B is surjective continuous and open and if B+ is
the integral closure of π(C +) in B.
A homomorphism ϕ : (A,A +)→ (B,B +) of aﬃnoid rings with B complete is called
topologically of ﬁnite type if there exist n∈ N0 and ﬁnite subsets T1,...,T n of A with
Ti·A open in A and a quotient mapping π : A⟨X1,...,X n⟩T1,...,Tn → B such that
ϕ =π◦ι, where ι : A→A⟨X⟩T is the canonical homomorphism.

open subring C of B+ such that B+ is integral over C, ϕ(A+)⊆C, and ϕ : A+→ C
is topologically of ﬁnite type.

a complete aﬃnoid ring B is topologically of ﬁnite type if and only if ϕ factors through
a quotient mapping A⟨X1,...,X n⟩→ B for some n∈ N0.
Proposition 8.46. LetA, B, and C be aﬃnoid rings with B and C complete and let
ϕ : A→B and ψ : B→C be continuous homomorphism of aﬃnoid rings.
(1) If ϕ and ψ are topologically o ﬁnite type, then ψ◦ϕ is topologically of ﬁnite type.
(2) If ψ◦ϕ is topologically of ﬁnite type, then ψ is topologically of ﬁnite type.

topologically of ﬁnite type over A.
(2) If f : Y →X is a morphism locally of weakly topologically space, then Y is stable
adic space.

9 Formal schemes as adic spaces
9.1 Formal (adic) schemes
Deﬁnition 9.1. A topological ring A is called pre-admissible if it satisﬁes the following
properties.
(a) A is linearly topologized (i.e., there exists a fundamental s ystem of neighborhood
of 0 consisting of ideals of A).
(b) There exists an open ideal I ⊆ A such that ( In)n tends to zero (i.e., for every
neighborhood of zero V there exists n≥ 1 with In⊆V ).
An ideal as in (b) is called ideal of deﬁnition. A is called admissible, if A is in addition
complete.
Remark 9.2. If I is an ideal of deﬁnition in a pre-admissible ring and if J is an open
ideal, then I∩J is again an ideal of deﬁnition. This shows that the ideals of d eﬁnition
in A form a fundamental system of open ideals.
Note that In is not necessarily open.
An adic ring is a topological ring A, such that an ideal I ⊆ A exists such that
(In)n∈N is a fundamental system of neighborhoods of zero. Such an ide al I is then an
ideal of deﬁnition of A.
Any adic ring is pre-admissible.
Recall the following properties (Remark 5.26).
Remark 9.3. Let A be a pre-admissible ring.
(1) The set of topologically nilpotent elements Aoo is an open ideal of A, and Aoo is
the union of all ideals of deﬁnition.
(2) For every ideal of deﬁnition I one has
Spec(A/I) = Spec(A/Aoo) ={ p∈ SpecA ; p open in A}.
In particular, the left hand side is independent of I.
Remark 9.4. Let X be a topological space. Let F be a sheaf of sets on X. If one
endows F (U ) with the discrete topology for all U ⊆ X open, then F is a presheaf
of topological spaces. This is not a sheaf of topological spa ces in general: If U is an
arbitrary open subset and (Ui)i is an open covering, then the discrete topology on F (U )
is in general not the coarsest topology making all restricti on maps F (U )→ F (Ui)
continuous.
93
===== PDF PAGE 94 =====
Now assume that the set B of open quasi-compact subsets of X is a basis of the
topology of X. Then it is easy to check that the restriction of F toB is a sheaf of
topological spaces. Thus there exists a sheaf F′ of topological spaces on X (unique up
to unique isomorphism) extending F|B. For every open subset U the underlying set of
F′(U ) is equal to F (U ), but the topology is not necessarily the discrete topology ifU
is not quasi-compact.
We call F′ the topologicalization of F .
If u : F→ G is a morphism of sheaves of sets, then u : F′→ G′ is a morphism of
sheaves of topological spaces.
The same considerations hold for sheafs of groups or of rings .
Now let A be an admissible ring. We attach a topologically ringed spac e (X, OX )
to A as follows. Let ( Iλ)λ be a fundamental system of ideals of deﬁnition in A. The
underlying topological space X is the subspace Spec( A/Iλ) of Spec( A) of open prime
ideals of A. Let Oλ be the topologicalization of OSpec(A/Iλ ) onX and set OX := lim
←−λ
Oλ
(projective limit in the category of sheaves of topological rings).
Deﬁnition 9.5. The topologically ringed space ( X, OX ) is called the formal spectrum
of A. It is denoted by Spf A.
Remark 9.6. Let A be a linearly topologized ring, ( Iλ)λ be a fundamental system of
ideals and let S⊆A be a multipicative set. Let Sλ be the image of S in Aλ :=A/Iλ.
Then the S−1
λ Aλ form a projective system with surjective transition homomo rphisms.
We denote by A{S−1} its projective limit.
If S ={fn ; n∈ N0} for f∈A, we write A{f}.
(1) The canonical homomorphism S−1A→ S−1
λ Aλ is surjective and has as its kernel
S−1Iλ. This shows that A{S−1} is the completion of S−1A for the topology given
by the fundamental system S−1Iλ.
In particular A{f} =A⟨1
f⟩.
(2) Let a⊆A be an open ideal. Then S−1a is an open ideal in S−1A. Its completion
is denoted by a{S−1}.
(3) If A is pre-admissible, then A{S−1} is an admissible ring. If I is an ideal of
deﬁnition, then I{S−1} is an ideal of deﬁnition of A{S−1}.
(4) If A is an adic with an ideal of deﬁnition I such that I/I 2 is a ﬁnitely generated
A/I-module (e.g., if I is a ﬁnitely generated A-module), then A{S−1} is an adic
ring and I′ :=I{S−1} is an ideal of deﬁnition. Moreover ( I′)n =In{S−1} ([EGA]
0I (7.6.11)).
(5) If A is adic and noetherian, then A{S−1} is adic and noetherian.
Remark 9.7. Let A be an admissible ring, X = Spf(A).
(1)X is a spectral space (because X ⊆ SpecA closed). For f ∈ A set D(f ) :=
D(f )∩X . Then (D(f ))f∈A is a basis of quasi-compact open subsets, stable under

(2) For all f∈A one has

Example 9.8. Let A be a ring endowed with the discrete topology. Then Spf A =
SpecA (as locally ringed spaces).
Deﬁnition 9.9. Let (X, OX ) be a topologically ringed space.
(1) It is called aﬃne formal scheme if it is isomorphic to Spf( A) for an admissible ring
A. An aﬃne formal scheme is called adic (resp. f -adic, resp. noetherian) if it is
isomorphic to Spf A, where A is an adic (resp. f -adic and adic, resp. noetherian
and adic) ring.
(2) An open subset U⊆X is called (adic, resp. f -adic, resp. noetherian) aﬃne formal
open, if (U, OX|U ) is an (adic, resp. f -adic, resp. noetherian) aﬃne formal scheme.
(3) (X, OX ) is called an (adic resp. f -adic resp. locally noetherian) formal scheme if ev-
ery pointx∈X has an open neighborhood that is an (adic resp. f -adic resp. noethe-
rian) aﬃne formal open.
It follows from Remark 9.7 that every formal scheme is a local ly ringed space.
Remark 9.10. By Remark 9.7 the f -adic (resp. noetherian) aﬃne formal open subsets
of an f -adic (resp. locally noetherian) formal scheme X form a basis of the topology
ofX . In particular if U ⊆X is an open subset, then ( U, OX|U ) is again an f -adic
(resp. locally noetherian) formal scheme.
Deﬁnition 9.11. A morphism of formal schemes is a morphism of locally and topo -
logically ringed spaces.
Let A, B be complete adic rings, X = Spf B,Y = Spf A, and let ϕ : A→ B be a
continuous homomorphism. Then aϕ := Spec(ϕ) maps Spf B into SpfA. Moreover, for
every f∈ A one has D(ϕ(f )) = aϕ−1(D(f )), and ϕ induces a continuous homomor-
phism A{f}→ B{ϕ(f )} compatible with restrictions corresponding by passage of f to
fg for some g∈A. Thus one obtains a homomorphism of sheaves of topological r ings
OY → aϕ∗OX which is easily seen to induce a local homomorphism on stalks . This
construction is compatible with composition of continuous ring homomorphisms and
we obtain a functor Spf from the category of complete adic rin gs into the category of
toplogically and locally ringed spaces.
Proposition 9.12. The functor Spf is fully faithful.

9.2 The adic space attached to a formal scheme
Deﬁnition 9.31. A complete aﬃnoid ring ( A,A +) is called sheafy if OSpa(A,A+) is a
sheaf of topological rings.
An adic ringA is called sheafy, ifA is complete,f -adic (i.e. it has a ﬁnitely generated
ideal of deﬁnition) and if the aﬃnoid ring ( A,A ) is sheafy.
A formal schemeX is called sheafy if for every aﬃne formal open subset U = Spf(A)
the adic ring A is sheafy.

Example 9.36. Let A be a complete valuation ring of height 1 endowed with its
valuation topology. Let m be its maximal ideal, k :=A/m, K := FracA.
ThenA is a γ-adic ring for all 0 ̸=γ∈ m. Set X := Spf(A) ={m} and OX (X ) = A.
Forx∈X := Spa(A,A ) with supp vx = m one has vx(¯a) = 1 for all ¯a∈k× (because
v(a)≤ 1 for all a∈A). Thus the only point x∈ Spa(A,A ) with supp( vx) = m is the
trivial valuation with support m.
For y ∈ Spa(A,A ) with supp vy = (0) one obtains an induced valuation vy on
K = Frac A. As vy is continuous (and (0) is not open in A), vy is not trivial. As
vy(a)≤ 1 for all a∈A,A is contained in the valuation ring Ay ofvy. As A is of height
1, it is maximal among all valuation rings ̸=K ofK and hence A =Ay. Thus the only
point y∈ Spa(A,A ) with supp(vy) = (0) is the valuation of A.
Clearly, y is a horizontal generization of x. One has OX(X) = O +
X (X) = A,
OX({y}) = FracA, and O +
X({y}) = A.

Lemma 9.38. LetA be an adic noetherian ring, I an ideal of deﬁnition. Let M , N be
A-modules and let N be ﬁnitely generated. Then every A-linear map M→N is strict

Proposition 9.39. The functor t : (lnSchf)→ (Adic) is fully faithful.
Proof. For a locally noetherian formal scheme Z setZt :={z∈t(Z) ; vz is trivial} and
letZ0 be the locally and topologically ringed space ( Zt, O +
t(Z)|Zt). By Remark 9.37 ((6))
the morphismσZ : Z0→Z is an isomorphism of locally and topologically ringed space s.
LetX andY be locally noetherian formal schemes and let f : t(X )→ t(Y) be a
morphism of adic spaces. As f is compatible with valuations, ona has f (Xt)⊆ Yt
and thus f induces a morphism of locally and topologically ringed spac esf0 : X0→Y0
which we consider via σX andσY as morphism g :X→Y of formal schemes. It is easy
to see that t(g) = f .
Let g1,g 2 :X→Y be morphisms of sheafy formal schemes with t(g1) = t(g2) =: f .
Then gi◦σX = σY ◦f0 for i = 1, 2 and hence g1 = g2. This shows that t is fully
faithful.
To determine the essential image of the functor t we make the following deﬁnition.
Deﬁnition 9.40. A full subcategory C of the category (Adic) of adic spaces is called
saturated if it satisﬁes the following properties.
(a) If an adic space X is isomorphic to Y∈C , then X∈C .
(b) If X∈C and U⊆X is an open subspace, then u∈C .
102
===== PDF PAGE 103 =====
(c) If X is an adic space that has an open covering ( Ui)i∈I with Ui∈C for all i∈ I,
then X∈C .
Clearly the intersection saturated subcategories is again a saturated subcategory.
Proposition 9.41. LetC be the smallest saturated subcategory of (Adic) such that
t((lnSchf))⊆C . Then the objects of C are those adic spaces X such that every x∈X
has an open aﬃnoid neighborhood U such that the following conditions hold.
(a) OX(U ) has a noetherian ring of deﬁnition A.
(b) OX(U ) is a ﬁnitely generated A-algebra.
(c) O +
X(U ) is the integral closure of A in OX (U ).
Proof. [Hu3] Prop. 4.2.

10 Rigid analytic spaces as adic spaces
Remark 10.1. Let k be a non-archimedean ﬁeld, let X be a scheme locally of ﬁnite
type over k and let X rig be the attached rigid analyit variety. Then the adic space
attached to X rig is isomorphic to the adic space X ad associated to the scheme X.
Remark 10.2. Let V be a complete discrete valuation ring (of height 1) and let k be
the ﬁeld of fractions of V . Let Ff be the category of formal schemes locally of ﬁnite
type over Spv V . Let Rk be the category of rigid analytic spaces over k. Finally let
E be the category of locally noetherian formal schemes whose m orphisms are the adic
morphisms between formal schemes.
Letta :E→ (Adic) be the functor X↦→t(X)a (note that because all morphisms are
adic, the respect to open subset of analytic points). Let rig :Ff→R k be the generic
ﬁber functor deﬁned by Raynaud and let u :Ff →E be the inclusion functor. Then
the diagram

The functor ta can be extended and described diﬀerently as follows (cf. [Hu4 ] 1.2.2).
The adic space S := Spa(V,V ) attached to Spf V consists of an open and a closed
point, and the canonical morphism S0 = Spa(k,V )→S is an open immersion onto the
open point (in particular it is a morphism of adic spaces of ﬁn ite type).

t(X )→ S and we can form the ﬁber product t(X )×S S0. Then Berthelot’s generic
ﬁber functor and the functor X↦→t(X )×SS0 are isomorphic.
11 Perfectoid spaces
In this section we explain a special class of adic spaces, nam ely the perfectoid spaces
introduced by P. Scholze. They allow to “tilt” from characte ristic zero to characteristic
p and vice versa in presence of high ramiﬁcation.
A ˇCech cohomology
Let X be a topological space. For a family of open subsets U = (Ui)i∈I we set
Ui0...iq :=Ui0∩···∩ Uiq
for all ( i0,...,i q)∈ Iq+1. If Y is a subspace of X we set U|Y := (Ui∩Y )i∈I . If
V = (Vj)j∈J is a second family of open subsets of X, we set
U×V := (Ui∩Vj)i∈I,j∈J.
IfU andV are open coverings of X, thenU×V is an open covering of X.
An open covering V = (Vj)j∈J of X is called reﬁnement of an open covering U =
(Ui)i∈I of X if there exists a map τ : J→I such that Vj⊆Uτ (j) for all j∈J.
Now let F be a presheaf of abelian groups on X. For an open covering U of X we
deﬁne the abelian group of q-cochains
ˇCq(U, F ) :=
∏
(i0,...,iq)∈I q+1
F (Ui0...iq ).
If F is a presheaf of rings, then ˇCq(U, F ) is an F (X)-module. For f∈ ˇCq(U, F ) we
denote byfi0,...iq its (i0,...,i q)-component. We call f alternating if for all permutations
π of{0, 1,...,q } one has
fiπ (0),...,iπ (q) = (sgnπ)fi0,...iq
and if fi0,...iq = 0 whenever the indices i0,...,i q are not pairwise distinct. The alter-
nating q-cohains form a submodule ˇCq
a(U, F ) of ˇCq(U, F ).
Forq≥ 0 we deﬁne dq : ˇCq(U, F )→ ˇCq+1(U, F ) by

One has a homomorphism

called the augmentation homomorphism.
LetV = (Vj)j∈J be a reﬁnement ofU and letτ : J→I be a map such that Vj⊆Uτ (j)
for all j∈J. Then τ induces homomorphisms of complexes τ• : ˇC•(U, F )→ ˇC•(V, F )
and τ• : ˇC•

ˇHq(X, F ) := lim

whereU runs through the set of open coverings of X, preordered by reﬁnement.
Deﬁnition A.1. LetU be an open covering of X. Then the covering U is called
F -acyclic if the the augmented ˇCech complex
0→ F (U )→ ˇC 0(U, F )→ ˇC 1(U, F )→ ˇC 2(U, F )→...
is exact. In other words, the augmentation morphism ε yields an isomorpism F (X) ∼→
ˇH 0(U, F ) and ˇHq(U, F ) = 0 for q≥ 1.
The trivial open covering U0 consisting of X only is always F -acyclic. An arbitrary
open covering U is always a reﬁnement of U0 (in a unique way) and U is F -acyclic if
and only if ˇC•(U0, F )→ ˇC•(U, F ) is a quasi-isomorphism. This implies in particular
the following remark.
Remark A.2. LetU andV be open coverings that are reﬁnements of each other. Then
U is F -acyclic if and only if V is F -acyclic.
The preceeding remark show that every open covering ( Ui)i of X such that there
exists i0∈I with Ui0 =X is F -acyclic.
Proposition A.3. Let F be a presheaf of abelian groups and let U = (Ui)i∈I and
V = (Vj)j∈J be open coverings of X such thatV|Ui0...i q is F -acyclic (or, more precisely,
F|Ui0...i q -acyclic) for all (i0,...,i q)∈Iq+1 and for all q≥ 0.
(1) Assume that also U|Vj0...j q is F -acyclic for all (j0,...,j q)∈ Jq+1 and all q≥ 0.
ThenU is F -acycic if and only if V is F -acyclic.
(2) IfV is a reﬁnement of U , then U is F -acyclic if and only if V is F -acyclic.
(3) ThenU×V is F -acyclic if and only if U is F -acyclic.
105
===== PDF PAGE 106 =====
Proof. Under the hypotheses in Assertion (1) one knows more general ly that ˇHq(U, F ) =
ˇHq(V, F ) for all q≥ 0. In particular (1) holds.
IfV is a reﬁnement of U , thenU|Vj0...j q and the trivial covering of Vj0...jq are reﬁne-
ments of each other. This shows (2).
Let us show (3). The coverings V|Ui0...i q and (U×V )|Ui0...i q are reﬁnements of each
other. Thus (U×V )|Ui0...i q is F -acyclic. Thus we may apply (2) to U and its reﬁnement
U×V .
Proposition A.4. Let B be a basis of the topology of X that is stable under ﬁnite
intersections. Let F′ be a presheaf of abelian groups on B and deﬁne a presheaf F on
X by setting for V ⊆X open
F (V ) = lim
←−
U⊆V,U∈B
F (U ).
Assume that for every U∈ B and for every open covering U = (Ui)i∈I of U by open
subsets Ui∈ B the presheaf F isU -acyclic, i.e., the augmented ˇCech complex
0→ F (U )→ ˇC 0(U, F )→ ˇC 1(U, F )→ ˇC 2(U, F )→...
is exact. Then F is a sheaf on X and for all q≥ 0 and for every open subset U of X
the canonical homomorphisms ˇHq(U, F )→Hq(U, F ) is an isomorphism. In particular
Hq(U, F ) = 0 for all U∈ B and q≥ 1.
Proof. The exactness of 0 → F (U )→ ˇC 0(U, F )→ ˇC 1(U, F ) shows that F′ is a sheaf
on B and thus F is a sheaf on X. The exactness of the augmented ˇCech complex
then shows that ˇHq(U, F ) = 0 for all q≥ 1 and for every open covering U of U∈ B
by open subsets in U . This shows ˇHq(U, F ) = 0 for all q≥ 1 and all U ∈ B and
thus a result of Cartan (e.g. [God] II 5.9.2) shows that ˇHq(U, F )→ Hq(U, F ) is an
isomorphism for every open subset U⊆X and all q≥ 0.
